But the New York Liie " was more sarcastic towards the South in general and Chicago in parti- cular than towards Mr. Besant's unlucky compari- son of himself to so great a man as " Jeames.” (This paper, by the way, is far wittier than our “Pi,nch," for which, indeed, it cultivates a lofty disdain.) A Spaniard of Irish extraction—curious pedigree —Couist, or more correctly Conde Morphy, holds with the Queen Regent of Spain exactly the same position as that of Sir henry ronsonby or Sir Fleetwood Edwards with our own Sovereign Queen Christina, during the present troublous times, has soley missed her private secretary, who is devoting a well-earned holiday to his pet hobby, musical composition. Conde Morphy is the naturalised de- scendant of one of the many Irish soldiers of for- tune who in the last century sold their swords and their services to the Catholic power of Spain.

Curiously enough beioie the present Spanish Ministry was iu office, the Queen, when at San Sebastian had as minister in attendance another Spaniard of Hibernian descent in the Duque de Tetuan, Don Carlos 0 Donnell, nephew and heir of the Marshal O'Donnell who some :30 years and more since subdued the rebellious Moors. The Al arshal's grandfather was the original exile from his native land, from which he was driven not only on account of his faith but also for his adherence to the Stuart cause. His grandson became a Spanish general,and at the close of the African campaign Queen Isabella created him Duke of Tetuan. On his death his title passed to the distinguished Foreign Secretary of the late Adminstration of Senor Canovas, a post which he attained after having passed through the diplomatic mill as Spanish Ambassador at Brussels and Vienna. The Duke is a grandee of Spain, and it goes without saying that he looks the part to perfection.

One of the busiest lady writen of the day is Mrs. L. T. Meade (Mrs. 11. Toulmin Smith). A native of Bandon, co. Cork, she came to London 17 years ago, and has since written between 50 and 60 books. Besides fulfilling her editorial duties as conductor of " Atalanta," Mrs. Meade writes on an average from three to four long stories a year. Unlike most authors, she did not experience much difficulty in making her way in the literary world of London, for her novels were readily accepted from the first.

Mrs. Meade married in 1879, tut continued to write under the name by which she was best known Iler earliest stories were written to all the cause of the suffering poor in the East-end, the most success- ful being " Scamp and I " and " Water Gypsis." That Mrs. Meade is a busy writer may be judged from the fact that., in addition to dictating her stories to a shorthand writer, she keeps two secre- taries constantly employed. Mrs. Meade is above the middle height, with dark hair, a pleasant face, brigbt. complexion, and a kindly and winning manner.

"CAPITA.L-" CHAT.

Mr. Gladstone,. as is well known, does not confine his attention to metropolitan second-bang booksellers, and last week he made a selection- from the catalogue of a Teddington dealer. Amongst the purchases were four rare books on Ireland, one of them a novel describing the state of the country a hundred years ago.

From to-day (Monday) the eyes of armed Europe will turn with interest to the little village of Coate, which Sir Evelyn Wood has selected for his head- quarters during the -manceavres. Thus associated with the throbbing of the drum and the rattle of musketry, the one great claim of the village to fame—that it was there that Richard.refferies lived and wrote—may be easily overlooKed. The cottage he lived in still stands, although the taint of modernness has destroyed much of its picturesque- ness. Even until to-day the favourite walk of the busy Swindenian is across .the fields that skirt the lake in the Manor House Park, which night and morning Jefferies walked when he fol- lowed the humbrum life ol reporter in alocal news- paper office, and the good village folk still talk of him without seeing the particular virtue of blades- cribing fields and hedgerows that anyone can see for the mere trouble of going so far.

The whole neighbourhood teams with interest, both historical and modern. Swindon itself is one of the most phenomenally developed towns in Eng- land, while hard by there is the great forest of Savernake, and the troops in Berkshire will be under the shadow of the birthplace at Wantage of Alfred the Great. In theintime the peaceful hills and vales of Wiltshire have seen actual war in its worst form, for round Devizes Clornivell's legions did some of their heaviest fighting, and a statue of the Madonna is still pointed out Were as having been hurled down by the Parliamentarians, but replaced by Divine hands during the night. Asa military manceuvring ground the whole district is unequalled

The announcement on Tuesday morning of the appointment of Sir Henry Norman as Viceroy of India, in succession to Lord Lansdowne, whose term of office expires in December, will cause general surprise. The post has so long been as- sociated with thoss.who have either distinguished themselves in civil administration or in the politi- cal arena that the policy of handing over this position, so fraught with heavy responsibilities, to one whose connection with civil administration only dates from 1883 (when he became Governor of Jamaica, and subsequently Queensland) will arouse much comment. From a military point or view, there are few men liiing, better fitted for the post, for Sir Henry's name has been prominent in mili- tary administration in India for the last half cen- tury. In 1844 his name appears as an ensign in the Bengal army, and during the troubles in the Pun- jaub that commenced four years later, and extended over a lengthy period, he so greatly distinguished himself that he was entrusted with the command of a detachment in the Sonthal campaign.

During the Mutiny he rendered honourable ser- vice and was frequently mentioned in despatches, and at its close he retired from the field, but con- tinued to serve in the military administration. In 1860 he was appointed Assistant Military Secre- tary at the House Guards; from 1862 to 1870 he was Secretary to the Government of India in the Mili- tary department, and he was afterwards for seven years Military Member of the Viceroy's Council lie then came home, and served from 1878 to 1883 at the India Office as Military Member in the Coun- cil of the Secretary of State. Since his career in civil administration Sir Henry has been fortunate in having his lot cast on peaceful times, but, never- theless, he has given proof of a delicate finesse in dealing with the ever-recurrent knotty problems cropping up in the colonies.

He was invited to make a tour of the Southern States, but declined because he could not forget that Thackeray never recovered from the champagne which he drank during a similar jour- ney. Whereupon one Yankee paper cruelly re- minded Mr. Besant that he, was not Thackeray.

A COSTLY DONKEY.

An amusing story comes from the Antipodes regarding Sir Robert Duff. On the occasion of the new Govenor visiting the Sydney Zoological Gardens his attention was directed to a donkey imported from the Soudan. One of the officials connected with the garden informed the Governor that the donkey in question was the moat ex- pensive animal in the Zoo, having cost somewhere about £370,000. On Sir RQbert inquiring how it came about that such a large sum was lavished on one animal, it was explained to him that the donkey was the only tangible thing the colony possessed to represent the sum spent on the Soudan expedition. It is added that Sir Eleory Parkes, who was one of the party, observed that the colony possessed other donkeys which had cost her even more than the Soudan one.

An outdoor bodice of rough red serge, with the revers and double capes edged with a very broad black braid, and a tight-fitting bodice, fastening over on one side with a belt of braid looks well. The braid also hems the plain skirt. Serge really threatens to be an immortal material; lb rules the womanhood of Britannia with an apparently Immutable sovereignty. Nothing very novel In autumn wraps has as yet put in an appearance. The moderately long coat, with close-fitting back and straight fronts fasten- ing with a double row of big pearl or horn buttons, is still much worn, and for travelling or country wear substantial-looking capes of reversible cloth, long enough to reach well below the hips, and having high collars, are fashionable. The new mackintoshes are exceeding smart, however; some have almost tight backs and double-breasted loose fronts, somewhat resembling a gentleman'. driving coat, with a deep cape lined with fancy cloth, while others are of striped silk (maokintoeh, of course) with a triple cape lined with check silk.

A new and stylish toilette is made of plain beige cloth, trimmed with cord gimp, for which fancy or plain silk may be substituted. The gored skirt is trimmed with four flounces, each gathered with a heading at the top and placed twoinches apart. The bodice is fastened invisibly with hooks in the centre of the front, which is trimmed with gimp from the shoulder scam to just below the bust. The overfronts are in handkerchief style, joined Into the shoulder and first underarm seam. The right side crosses over the left and passes round to the centre of the back, where it is fastened under a ribbon bow. The high collar is trimmed with gimp. The sleeves are ornamented at the wrist with gimp, and the deep puff is gathered at the arm- hole and again just above the elbow. Yours lovingly, JENNIE

A corresdondent of the " Daily Chronicle," who has recently paid a visit to Miss Sprules, whose family for over a century has been engaged in the industry of lav- ender distillation at Mitcham, writes :—The process of distillation is a very interesting and somewhat primitive affair. The lavender is deftly cut by men with small saw-like scythes, then made up into sheaves, and finally rolled into " mats" into which they are secured by skewers. These mats or sacks of lavender are then brought along to the distillery, which consists of a lower floor, in which are the receiving cans for the oil and the , furnaces for heating the stills above, and an upper floor which is a raised platform of wood with a thatched roof supported by beams, in which are the stills and vats. The big iron coppers or stills are filled with the pretty delicate looking bloom by men naked to their waist,who press it down and stamp upon it till the still is tightly packed. Water is then added, thA head of the still is firmly fixed on by means of a crane, and the whole made air tight (to prevent the escape of any steam) by layers of whitening. The men then light the fires below and the vapour, passing through a pipe technically known as the" worm "is caught in a huge vat nearly full of cold water, where it gets condensed,and flows into a receiving can below as oil and water. The essential oil is thus retained, whilst the water. which is useless trickles away. In the distillation of peppermint the water is collected, as it is regarded by the poorer people of the district as a potent remedy against certain minor ailments. A large portion of the lavender oil is sent up , by Miss Sprules to the druggists for medicinal purposes, the remainder being retained for the preparation of their famous lavender water, the details of which are her own secret. During the autumn and winter, the bott- ling of the lavender water, lavender essence, and the aromatic lavender salts, is carried on in the farm par- lour. The stranger should not leave this attractive place without a stroll through the fields where the lavender and mint grow. A huge field of lavender lies a little to the east of the town, skirted on one side by a plain of yellow waving oats and on the other by a field of mauve tinted mint. Near by is a field of camomile, the white blossom of which is dried and used in the composition of various drugs.

Then, without being missed, they passed into the great conservatory. "I have something to say to you„ Lord Chilton," said Miss Norreys, as they passed down the flower. bordered, fragrant aisles to tho group of luxuriant palm trees that reared their plumed crests to the very top of the central dome. "I have a project on hand, and I desire your assistance and co- operation." "You have but to command me, madam," said the young viscount. "Does your project concern Miss Winter ?"

The lady sighed heavily. "No," she acknowledged wearily, "I wish to speak to you about Lord liollarkwood." Lord Chilton smiled significantly. "You wish to announce your engagement to marry him?" " No. I shall not marry him. I shall never marry," cried Miss Norreys, with passionate em- phasis. "But Lord Darkwood lia.s done a wrong which I desire to right. Listen." She told him rapidly of Mr. Barsby's mission and its result, of her belief that Cord Darkwood lived a prisoner in his own dungeons and she added— "I have planned to search these dungeons to- night secretly. Mr Sutton will take charge of the expedition. I shall go, of course. Will you accompany us ?" The viscount was amazed at her revelation, and horror-stricken at her suspicious of the present holder of the inarquisate. But lie could not refuse to join the expedition she had planned, little as a burglarious entrance to any man's premises as to his taste.

"I will go," he said gravely. "If this thing be true if Edward Lord Darkwood lives, he will owe his freedom to you, Miss Norreys. But for you he might have perished, his fate unsuspected. As for me, I beiieve him dead. Captain Tollish would not have allowed him to live, if the captain is the vil- lian you think him. May I ask the reason of your strange interest in. the marquis who has been be- lieved dead? Why have you searched out his fate with all this care and painstaking? Is it that you doubted this man who desires to marry your'

The heiress turned sharply towards him, and he saw that her mouth was strangely drawn, and a tortured expression gleamed in her eyes. "Lord Chilton," she exclaimed in a low, passion- ate voice, "these two men, cousins, both in their turn Marquises of "Darkwood since then, once cruelly and foully wronged an innocent girl. They killed her. Between them they killed her. And I, knowing her fate, swore to avenge her. Captain Tollish was the more guilty of the two. Upon his head must fall my bitterest revenge. That revenge must be the deprivation a rank, wealth, honours, luxuries. He shall be transferred into plain Captain Tollish again, with' debts and all, and my revenge upon him shall be complete." A strange light irradiated that pale olive face, and glowed in the velvety-brown eyes like fire. "My-revenue upon the other will be his release from imprisonment—his restoration to life, liberty, And all that he bolds dear. Did he but know tä whom he would owe all these, you would see that my revenge would be coals of fire upon his head." "You ..hate them both ?" asked the viscount, wonderingly. "Hate them I" How her face shone; how hard and cold it grew, like.marble. And then how suddenly her features quivered with a quick agony, and the hardness melted like snow in the sun, and a strange tender- ness and anguish mingled in her expression, only to yield to the returning coldness. "Yes, I hate them both ! They killed her— killed her I But I will avenge her." "My dear Miss Norreys— " "Pardon me, Lord Chilton, I cannot bear remon- strance. lam what I am, and nothing can change me. Ido not know why I have said so much to you. I shall complete my revenge to-night," she said, wearily. "And to-morrow I shall go away. My guests will leave to-morrow, and I shall go to London with them. I shall not return to Beech- moat. I shall offer the place for sale. When my revenge shall be accomplished my life will virtually be ended. I will assist you to find Miss Winter. I should like to witness your marriage to her, and then I shall leave England or join a sisterhood, I do not yet know which. All this in confidence, my lord. Now let us return to our friends. Remember that we must leave Beechmont upon our expedition at eleven o'clock."

They returned to the drawing-room, MissNorreys with recovered composure. They had scarcely joined the nearest group when a servant entered sad anuotuaood that a 110:01011 hauled Craft dasize4

CEIAPTEIt LXIV. NOT TILE WOMAN. Mr. Inspector Craft was standing in the morn- ing4oom in a waiting attitude When Lord Chilton entered. The detective officer was a small, sandy- haired man, with eyes keen as a ferret's, yet .of a I. peculiarly quiet and unobtrusive mien. The young viscount had never seen him, and lie was conscious of a sense of disappointment. Was this the famed detective who had unearthed a dozen celebrated mysteries, who had disentangled certain enigmas which had puzzled all England, and discovered and hunted down, despite all his precautions and dis- . guises, the principal in the noted Prior murder 'case? The inspector smiled faintly. He read the via. count's thoughts. - , "1 am Lord Chilton," said our hero, quietly. "Are you come to tell me, Mr. Inspector, that the task I have set you is toe hard? lam well aware of all the difficulties in the way. .So many years I having elapsed, it must be well-nigh impossible to discover the identity of that ill-fated woman." " I have harder tasks, my-lord," said the inspec- tor, "though I do not deny that the lapse of years made this difficult. I have completed my work. have discovered the name Of the woman who Is buried in Penistone churchyard, and I am provided with proofs of her identity." . Lord Chilton's heart seemed almost to stand still. An indefinable dread came upon him. Who was she—this mother of Gwen—the poor young creature who had perished so miserably on the Yorkshire wastes so many years ago? He was conscious of a wish that he had made no effort to discover her name. "At the coroner's inquest at the time her body was discovered," said Mr. Craft, "a complete description of her person and apparent age were recorded in the coroner's register. Also a descrip- tion of her clothing, such -as it was. I copied these descriptions at the outset. One or two 'personal peculiarities of the deceased woman greatly simplified my task. It was recorded that one joint of the first finger of her ?eft hand was absent. Also that two incisor, teeth in her upper jaw were missing. Also that her collar-bone had once been broken, and that, probably through lack of care at the period of its fracture,. there remained and was seen upon her person a protuberance as of the end of a broken bone. With this data I set to work." "Every person has some personal peculiarity. Nearly every person has lost teeth: The marks appear to have been too trifling to assist in identifi- cation." " Nothing is too trifling for the consideration of men in my line of business, my lord. A straw is sufficient to show which way the wind blows.. It is not necessary that I should describe to you my mode of operation. I have, of course' many re- sources and aids known only to myself. You desire to know the result of my work, not the manner in which the work was accomplished," said the detec- tive officer. "I will say, however, that I adver- tised freely throughout the kingdom. I searched the files of old newspapers, old police records— everything that could possibly aseist me. And it was through an old newspaper that I obtained a clue to the mystery." ' " How so "In an old Manchester newspaper, bearing date February 3rd, 1857,1 found an advertisement; but here is the newspaper. Read the notice for your- self, my lord." . The detective handed to Lord Chilton itn ancient newspaper, yellowed by time, and slightly defaced with use. A notice in an advertising column, outlined by pencil marks, had been folded in plain view. Lord Chilton took the paper and read as fol- lows : "ilO REWAR.D.—For information leading to the discovery of Mary Graham' ' wife of Adelbert Gra- ham of Manchester, who left her home January 27th. Is supposed to have set out on foot for Don- caster, where she had relatives. Was suffering from aberration of mind. Said Mary Graham was eighteen years of age, tall, slim, with black hair and eyes, flighty in her manner, violent in her temper. Wore an old silk gown, Paisley shawl, and straw hat trimmed with black velvet. Had lost two front teeth in upper jaw, and lisped in her speech. liad lost one joint to first finger of left hand. Any information leading to her recovery will be gratefully received and paid for by her afflicted litaand, No. 9, Dovewell-street, Man- chester." The young viscount was startled at the coinci- dence of this description with that of the woman who had been found 'upon the Lone Moor. "After, finding that advertisement, I made a search • for Adelbert Graham," said the inspector. "I found him still in Manchester, a baker by trade, .married to a buxom woman, and father to several children. He had believed his first wife to be dead, and had married again ten years ago; but still he was haunted by the fear that the first wife might still be living, and that she might turn up some day and ruin his happiness for ever, lie is devoted to his present family, and that fear of his first wife was like a constant nightmare to him. When I told him my errand, he welcomed me as a brother. I left him in a perfect transport of delight, the happiest man in England !" He believed that woman, thew, to be his wife ?" "1 proved her to be such," replied the detective. "This Adelbert Graham was born and brought up in Manchester. In visiting Doncaster on business he saW and fell in love with a pretty young girl, whom he soon after married. The girl had long been subject to fits. After her marriage she became moody, suspicious, and violent. Her mind" became utterly deranged. In a fit of anger against her husband, she left her home, and he never saw her again. He searched almshouses and hospitals, he went to Doncaster, but he found no trace of her. After a few years he made up his mind that she was dead, and contracted his second marriage; but afterwards, when the children began to come, he grew terrified lest the first wife should be still living, and should come to claim her rights. I took his sworn statement in regard to Mary Graham. I went to Dnneaster, found her relatives, and took their sworn statement; and to be brief, my lord, I have fully proved the occupant of that grave marked ' Magdalen ' in Peuistone churchyard to be the wife of Adelbert Graham." Lord Chilton sat silent, his face very grave. Had he discovered Gwen's origin at last. Her mother had been subjects' to. fits, and her father was a baker in Manchester with a large family ! But sud- denly his face brightened. "You are mistaken, inspector ?" he exclaimed. "This Mary Graham left her home in Jane:lcy, 1857. The woman who perished on the moor came to Lonemoor in November, 1856, and then left Lonemoor just one month later. You see that you are mistaken I" "Not so, my lord?" "But the woman gave birth to a child in Lone. moor in November !" "Not this woman, my lord. There were two women," said the inspector. "One woman come to Lonemoor, gave birth to a child as you say, and disappeared a month later; but she was not the woman whose body was found on the moor." "Not the same woman !" " By no means, my lord !" "Then where is she 1" "That you did not employ me to search out," said Mr. Craft. "I don't know what became to her. But _one thing I do know—she got away safely in spite of the storm, the cold, and her weakness. That woman may be living yet, my lord I" The viscount aarted. The idea had never occurred to him before. "The devil takes care of his own, my lord," said the inspector. "He took carp of that girl that night; he's probably takieg care of her still. She was either a bad lot, or crazy, or both. She is probably in some almshouse or asylum. Would you wish me to find her ?" "No—no !" said Lord Chilton with a shudder. "Not at present, at least." "The body of this woman has been taken up," said Mr. 'Craft redectively. "Her husband and relatives identified the bones by the marks I have mentioned, and Mr. Graham had them removed to Manchoster only yesterday. He will pat up a suitable tablet with her real name upon it. He intends to make the fact of her death apparent to everyone beyond all doubt." The viscount was still sile-it. He had much to think of. Doubt, perplexity assailed him. "Mr. Graham and I drov'e ore: to the house at Lonemoor yesterday morning," continued Mr. Craft. THREE FORTTItTATE WOMEN.

We will introduce you to the oldest one first. Her ' name is Mrs. Jessie Russell, and shelives near the City of New York, in America. She was born in London in 178? and is therefore 105 years old. She remembers many incidents of her childhood, and still has traces of former beauty. Her eyes are bright, and become animated when she talks. She retains her mental faculties com- pletely. She descends two flights of stairs tl every mea/t• and returns without assistance. Up to three years ago she was a regula; attendant at the Greenwood Baptist Church. Her sight is excellent, and she reads the news- papers every day and takes great interest in the news from England. She has never had any severe illness in her life, and her appetite and digestion are almost as good as they were fifty years ago. The second woman says : " Ever since I was a child 1 have suffered from illness. Thirteen years ago a tired,. languid, and heavy feeling came over me. The whites of my eyes became tinged with yellow, my skin was sallow, and my hands and feet were cold and clammy. My mouth tasted badly, especially in the morning, and I was often sick, vomiting a sour, frothy fluid. I had a pain in the chest and bides, heartburn, and flatulency. For four years I suffered like this, and I shall be glad to recommend the medicine that finally cured me.". (Signed) (Mrs.) FaatacKs EMILY SMITH, 49e, Wocdhouse- street, Leeds, Jauuary 25th, 1892.

The third woman says : " I have been delicate all Day life. For the past six years I have always felt tired, languid, and weak. I had a poor appetite, a blci taste In the mouth, and pain after every morsel I ate. I waa dizzy, and otten felt as if I was going to fall. I always felt sick, and would retch and strain, but could bring nothing up. There was a dull pain at the right side and a dreacful pain between the shoulders. I never knew what it was to be well. After being on my feet for a time my legs would swell very much. No doctor was able to help me, nor any medicine; that is, up to the time I took the one which gave me my health back. / never felt so well ia my life as I do now." (Signed) (Mrs.) SARAH SHARMAN, Glinton, near Market Deeping• February 19th, 1892.

Why do we put these three women in a group—the one venerable woman in America and the two others here in England? They are perfect strangera to one another and always will be. We do it to show how long and happily a woman may live if she only happens to escape the malady that everywhere threatens her sex ; and also to show that those who have been burdened and cursed with it may be cured and once ,more taste the joys of health.

Mrs. Smith concludes her letter in these wordS "After taking three bottles of Mother Seigel's Curatiiee Syrup all pain and sickness left me, and I knew nomore of the ailment that had troubled me so long, indigestion and d 3 spepeia. I know others who have been benefited by the Syrup. AU sufferers should use it. Mrs. Sharman adds that after she had doctored and doctored and taken everything almost without getting any good from it. Mr. Webstes, a grocer of Minton, said," Why don't you use Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup ?" She acted on his suggestion, and health wasn't long in coming. "I cannot speak too highly of Seigel's Syrup," she says. We sheuld agree with her in that opinion, for a remedy that will in a few weeks put an end to a case of chronic indigestion and dyspepsia is certainly worth a good Iv( ra or two.

Now here you have these three Englishwomen—oce so fortunate as to have lived more than a century with- out an illness ; the others (still more foetu)zate) have known the sadness of suffering and the pleasure of re-- covery. Oh, my ! oh my, how hard it is to tell who is best of in this queer world,

LADIES' LETTER

lay DEAREST NELLIE,

It would appear that large hats are to be super- seded by small ones, and that the toque and the tiny bonnet are to share the favour of the modish multitude. Such a pretty little toque did I Bee recently. It was a square shape made of chip straw, across the front was a huge bow with a choux of pale rose pink in the centre and two jet pins and an osprey, and at the baok was a group of black roses. Some pretty Inidesmaids' hats had black lace brims edged with a silver and light blue trimming, the crowns being made entirely of this trimming and decked with a wreath of yellow roses brought beneath the brim to rest on the hair at one side. Another pretty hat was of white chip, plateau shape, trimmed with pink audyellow roses and a large bow of green ribbon.

A "fled Riding Hood" costume for a mite of two to tour years is in scarleta faced cloth and is prepared for autumn wear. The shape is exceedingly simple, and is cut almost in "smock" pattern, the trimming consisting of bands of black watered silk ribbon brought from the shoulder seams and fastened over the chest in a rosette. Two bands of the same ribbon orna- ment either sleeve, and the costume is completed with a bonnet of the same cloth, trimmed and tied with the watered silk. This mantle would copy well in holland.

In making washing dresses, especially those of lawn or gingham, that shrink so badly in the first and second washings, allow for a tuck in the skirt, and when it is finished put an inch and a half tuck on the wrong aide of the hem. Cut the waist to allow for seams one inch larger than usual on shoulder and under the arm. Do not stitch these seams, but sew securely by hand to give the proper fit; then machine-stitch them half an inch nearer the edge of the seam. Make the sleeves one inch longer than usual. Sew on the facing and turn the sleeve up the extra length, turning the facing under correspondingly. Make the belt one inch longer, turning in half the extra length at each end. In putting in the sleeve, lay a half-inch plait where it joins the under-arm seam, and have the gathers quite full on top. When ready for the wash, take out the temporary tucks and after- wards it will be found to be in proper proportion, and no one will be the wiser for the letting-out process. If the skirt is finished with one or more ruffles, take the tuck on the wrong side just above the ruffle, so that the heading will hide the seam.

To Clean Old Bed Ticks.

Wash the bed-tick, previously shaking well free of Huff and small feathersokc. Rinse in plenty cf cold water, in which a teaspoonful of permangante of potash has been dissolved. If there are any stains take them out with a weak solution of chloride of lime and water, or powdered starch and water made intoa thin paste and laid on the stains, and brushed off when dry. When the bed-tick is thoroughly dry and aired, wax the Inside by rubbing well with a piece of beeswax to prevent the feathers working through.

===== 0002601_18930909_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
Sweet .Ripe Tomatoes Pickles.

Choose for this small pear yellow tomatoes, or small, round, smooth red tomatoes. Do not peel, but prick the skins all over with a line needle. To 7lbs. of the toma- toes take 3lbs. vf white sugar, one quart of vinegar, one teaspoonful of salt, one teaspoonful of allspice, one tablespoonful of ginger, one tablespoonful of cloves, one of mace one of cassia buds, and two of mustard seed, and liall a dozen pepper-corns Put the vinegar and sugar together over the fire, boil and skim thoroughly, then put in the fruit. Put the spices, which should be all unground„ into a muslin bag, and put them on the top of the tomatoes. Simmer all very slowly until the tomatoes are .quite soft, then take from the fire, and cool and put away. It has been my custom for years to cook all toinatoe pickles in stone jars and set them in the preserve closet in those same jars.

Light FlakV Pastry. There is an art in making light pastry, as in every- thing else. Some people are what is called " heavy- headed," and can never make goad pastry, while to others the gift seems a natural one. Still, much can be done by practice. The following is a special method : Take a pound of the best flour, rub it through a sieve, place it a little distance from the fire for a short time to get thoroughly dry ; then rub in the half of three- quarters of a pound of butter and the juice of half a lemon, mix it lightly up together with a little water. Roll it out as thin as a crown piece, put a layer of butter all over it, double it is four, and roll it out again ; do this twice, by which time all the butter Will be in. Then fold it up and put it down on a plate on the stones of the larder to gct cool. After this roll it out three times and it is fit for use.

A TALE OF LONEUOOR.

BY AIRE. HARRIET LEWIS. Author .of ."Her _Double_ " Old Lifel

Shadows," "Lady Kildare," etc.

CIIAPTER Lx,an. REVENGE.

By the time Mr. Barsby returned, and was shown up to Miss Norreys's bboudoir, the lady of Beech- mont had completely won over the lawyer to her .opinion, and they had fully settled upon a plan of action even to its smallest details. The bailiff was required to repeat „his story. Mr. 'Sutton cross-examined him upon every point, and a conviction that Lord Darkwood was entombed, either living or.dead, in the .dungeons beneath the castle ruins, took fall and firm possession of the lawyer. Yet, for the reasons already given, Mr. Sutton adhered to his desire that the investigation should be conducted secretly. Miss Norreys disliked the publicity of legal proceedings, and it was thought that the search could be conducted without excit- ing sensation or even notice. 'I will undertake to secure the co-operation of Lord Chilton," said Miss Norreys. " And you, Mr. Sutton, must secure the aid of the butler. I, With my friends will be at tho park entrance of the ruins as the clock strikes the hour of midnight. That is all, _I _ _

It was all. Mr. Sutton took his leave, and slowly .retterned to Dunhohn Castle, his face stern yet calm, his mind fixed in the resolve to probe this matter to some conclusion. His heart thrilled with a longing to behold again the master he had believed dead. He was full of agitation and unrest under that outward calmness, full of eagerness and_ex- citement. It seemed to him that he could never wait until midnight to know the truth, and yet a heavy gloom bung over his spirit. . "If Captain Tallish met him and did him any wrong, he surely killed him !" he thought. " Cap- tain Tollish —I cannot call him Lord Darkwood in my own mind now—is not a man to do anything in a half-way manner. If he desired to rid himself of his cousin, he would have done so decisively and effectually—by murder I If we find any trace ot our dear lord down there, it will be his grave I" He was very silent and very gloomy throughout the dinner. Lord Darkwood was still elated, how- ever, and did not notice his moodiness. His lord- ship drank more wine than usual, told more Stories, and, when the butler had departed, talked. confi- dentially over the walnuts and the wine of -Miss Norreys, her family, her fortune, and her wonder- ful beauty, declaring his love for her, and asserted that the day that made him her husband would see him the happiest man in England. Alter dinner Mr. Sutton withdrew to the library, and meditated . some plan by which he could approach the pompous old butler upon the subject in hand.

Fortune favoured his design. Lord Darkwood chanced to have.an engagement at Shrewsbury with one of the agents whom he had secretly employed to search for Gwen, and whom he did not wish seen .at the eastie, and he ordered a carriage and went upon his errand. • During his Absence Mr. Sutton went to the but- ler's pantry, found that functionary, and obtained a private interview. with him. We need not record its details, nor describe the horror and amazement 'of the old servant. ft is enough to say that he entered into the scheme with heart and soul, and undertook to obtain the keys of the ruins from their repository in the library. Meanwhile, Miss Norreys was the life of her own pleasant circle. She had never been gayer than to-night. She uttered brilliant witticisms and bon mots, she was thoughtful of. otLers, so that her lightest words could not wound: She had never seemed more radiant, yet all the while she was racked with anxieties and tortured with a profound and horrible despair. After dinner the party adjourned to the draw- ing room. .Miss Kenright took up her woollen embroidery, and Colonel 'Warburton proffered his services in holding her skeins. Sir William Ensor and Miss Mifly Kenright retreated to the grand piano. Mrs. Kenright lolled in a great chair, and began the rehearsal to Miss Ensor of some episode of her youth, in which the baronet's sister seemed greatly interested. Lord Chilton seemed left by common consent to the care of his hostess, and she took his arm and walked slowly the length of the great room several titnes. _ _

to speak with Lord Chilton. The person named Craft had been shown into the morning-room.

This was the detective officer whom Lord Chilton had commissioned .to search out the history and mystery of Gwen's parentage. He had finished his task and come with his report to his.employer. The Niscount hastened to the morning room.

===== 0002601_18930909_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
keeper who bad idtintilied Mrs. Graham's body as that of the vagrant whom she bad nursed, and we proved to her beyond all shadow of doubt that she had been mistaken. Such a scene as followed! The old woman went into a fit, and a doctor was sent for in hot haste. The butler showed symptoms of going into a fit also. He laughed and"cried like a woman, and we left him in a state of mind bor- dering on imbecility. There was some mystery in the case of that 'vagrant," concluded Mr. Craft, meditatively:. "If • I could spare the time, I'd bunt out the matter for my own satisfaction." "I :may set you the task a little later," said Lord Chilton. " At present I have other Work for you. The child who was born at Lonotoor seven years ago has grown to lovely girlhood, is known as Miss Myner, and • has been for some months employed as governesito the Lady Geor- gina Charteris, Lord Darkwood's daughter. Misr Myner has recently disappeared. I believe thai she has gone to some remote place under another name. I want to find her."

He told the whole story briefly, showed Gwen's unsigned letter, and answered the interrogatories of the inspector, who looked grave and thoughtful. " "Before engaging upon this new affair," said the. viscount, "let us settle existing amounts. You. have done your work well, Mr. Inspector. Let us hope you will succeed equally well with the new problem. • Lorcl Chilton proceeded to a writing-table, took a blank cheque from his notebook, and filled it in for an amount so generous that Mr. Inspector Craft, whose • boast it was that nothing ever astonished himi stared in actual surprise, and muttered his thanks in a bewildered sort of way. The viscount presently dismissed him, and the detective sauntered away to his quarters at the Darkwood Arms in Dunhohn village. The evening wore away slowly. Coffee was • brought in at ten o'clock. At eleven the bedroom candles were ordered, and the guests departed to their rooms—all but Lord Chilton.

Miss Norreys went to her dressing-room, and returned a few minutes later with a long black silk circular cloak round her, her dress looped up to - walking length, a hat on her head, and, oddly enough, a thick veil thrown over her hat, and so arranged that at a touch it would fall over her • face.

No cme had seen her return to the drawing-room, the hall porter having been temporarily absent from his post. _ _

"We will go out at one of these windows, my lord," said the heiress. "Myra will admit us- into the honks by a private •entrance upon our return."

'She apProachea one of the long French win4ows, opened it, and passed out upon the terrace. Lord Chilton followed her.

The night was dark, but not unpleasant. In the gloom objects could be distinctly traced at a dis- tance of several feeL

"No one knows, except Naya and Aga, that we are out," said Miss Norreys, pausing by a tall gar- den vase. "The Servants jll close the house , directly. Ali! here is Aga !" she exclaimed, as, a dark little figure approached them from the direc- tion Of the park. "1 will take him with us." "Where is Mr. I3arsby ?" "Near the small park entrance, to which I have the key. I could not take a carriage at this hour without exciting gossip in my household, and the distance to the castle—three miles—is too long to walk. Mr. Barsby has' a two-seated waggonette which he will have in waiting for us. He is' probably waiting now." Miss Norreys and Lord Chilton walked rapidly, down the wide avenue followed by Aga. They crossed' a corner of the park and passed out at the entrance the lady had designated. Mr. Barsby was in wait- ing, as expected. They entered his waggonette and drove through Dunholm village, halting in the highway at a point nearest Dunholm Castle. Here they alighted, obtained entrance into' the castle grounds through a small gate which the castle butler had left open for their use, and then slowly threaded' the park in the direction of the ruins. "Does your courage fail, Miss Norreysl" asked` the viscount. ',

She turned her pale olive face towards him, and. he saw her eyes glow through the darkness. "No V' she answered. A thousand times no I could not turn back if I would 1 My lord, if knew that death waited for me there among those grim old ruins, I would still goon! It seems as if I were driven on by some power beyond and greater than: myself. lam going to my revenge 1"

FINANCE COMMITTEE.. This Committee recommended the adoption, of the tender of Chatwood's Patent Safe and Lock Company Ltd., for a safe for the office of the rate collectors,. at £35. The confirmation of the minutes was moved by Councillor Slum GREEN, seconded by Councillor TitisLEY. Councillor SHAW before the minutes were passed,referred • to the resolution that in future the auditors be paid accord- ing to statute. He thought it rather strange being proposed by the vice-chairman of the Finance Committee, who some time ago was in the field for the position of audi- tor for the borough. He felt sure that this resolution could not have been brought forward and sent to the Council that night to be passed unless there had been some feeling other than pleasant in the mind of Mr. Shaw Green ("nonsense.") He did not see why they should alter the position of the auditors until the time arrived when a public auditor should be appointed. He would more an amendment that the minutes of the Finance Committee be passed with the exception of the resolution in question. Councillor DixoN seconded the amendment.. Councillor SHAW GREEN in answer to Councillor Srakw said the Cou.ncil some months ago passed a resolution that the auditors be pail a certain amount. The amount had been paid to them in pretty even sums, though he regretted to say that he read something of Councillor Muir saying that he had had no part. Councillor Mum I said nothing of the sort. I Was— The CHAIRMAN : Councillor Shaw Green is speaking. Councillor Mum t I thought he had finished. Councillor SHAW' GREEN (continuing) The sum ar- ranged to be paid to the elective auditors was £75. The sum could not be legally made up in the way in which it was introduced, and no member of the Committee wi,hed to be surcharged for making an illegal pay- ment. Councillor Muse did not know whether it was with any vindictive spirit that Councillor Shaw Green turned round and grinned at him. (Laughter.) He had only to tell Councillor Shaw Green that he had also no feel- ing in the matter. He was rather pleased that the Finance Committee had come to such a wise conclu- sion. . . .

The CHAIRMAN thought that the wording of the reso- lution should recommend it. As long as the auditors were paid according to statute, they could not get wrong. Councillor SHAW eventually withdrew his amendment and the minutes of the Committee were passed unani- mously.

STREET IMPROVEMENT COMMITTEE.

The CHAIRMAN proposed the adoption of this Commit- tee's minutes, which were published in last week's "Examiner."—Councillor Mum seconded.—Councillor DIXON drew attention to the fact that there was no light on one side of the Post Office. Near the Post Office at night, it was so darlrthat people could scarcely see. The minutes as amended were passed unanimously.

HEALTH COMMITTEE. This Committee contained the following resolution, " that Ralph Norman, John Church, and James Powell be respectively appointed Inspectors of Provisions under and for the purposes of the Town's Improvement Clauses Arts and the Warrington Improvement and Market Act, 1854. The CHAIRMAN in proposing the adoption of the minutes of this committee gave a report of his visit to the recent congress on health and sanitary matters, held at Edinburgh. He also drew attention to the valuable information contained in the annual report for 1891-2, of the medical officer of health. Be said that they had now got entirely rid of the smallpox. They had not a single cafe in the hospital and to their knowledge there was not a single case in the borough. During the past week the Union Jack had been flying on the Hope Hospital in celebration of the welcome departure ot the enemy. May they have seen the last of it !—(hear, hear)—and have no further acquaintance. They had every reason to congratulate themselves at so quickly getting rid of the disease, because whatever might be said to the contrary, and great deal had been said, there could be no doubt that, considering that they were totally unprepared for the outbreak, it was entirely owing to the efforts of the Health Committee, the medical officer of health and his son, and the other medical men of the town, that the disease had been con- quered in the space of about 13 months from the time it made its appearance. He would now give an example of the inconsistency of some people. Notwithstanding the outbreak of smallpox, and scarlet fever, the death rate in the borough in the year ending December 31, 1892, was only 221 per thousand, which was a very satisfactory average. Although the public got se excited with regard to the smallpox, that was the condition last year. What was the present condition of the borough. During the present quarter, extending so far fora period of nine weeks, the death rate had been equal to 35 per thousand, and 18 per thousand of that had resulted from diarrhcea. Durine, these nine weeks there had been 167 deaths from diarrhosa,and yet there had not been a single complaint from anybody. He was glad to say that diarrhoea was abating. All the nurses had been discharged from the Hope Hospital, and there was now only the caretaker and his wife left. With regard to keeping it on, no definite decision had been arrived at, but it would be better to maintain it to provide for any further cases which might breakout or were likely to be imported frosts some other town. Councillor FLOOD seconded the adoption of the minutes. Councillor PLIMPTON called the attention of the Com- mittee to the great nuisance of Sankey Brook. Since the last extension of the borough, the boundary includes a portion of the brook. There was a great amount of building near the brook in Wellfield-street, and for the past few weeks, the stench from it had been simply unbearable. He had experienced it in his own house. He hoped that the Committee would pay some attention to the matter. Councillor BEAMAN referring to the recent congress said that he had learned something by attending it. He then gave a detailed and exhaustive account of the various contrivances for sewerage and distinctions. He was glad that the Hope Hospital was free, but they had now the alarming news from the East coast that cholera had invaded the shores of England, and it behoved the sanitary authorities of this town to take all measures to keep that foul enemy from our midst. Councillor HILL said that they had had an elaborate report of the visitation to the congress, but what about the expense? He would like to know the cost of the deputation. He f-und that there were a number of houses marked on the minutes as unfit for habitation. He thought that the medical officer or some one ought to , tell the people who owned these houses, what was re- ' quired to be done so that they could be put into a sanitary condition. The owners ought not Co be under persecution. 'The CHAIRMAN said that every information necessary was given to property owners. Councillor ROBERTS would like to contradict most emphatically the idea of any persecu-i tion to any owners of property. There were many members who themselves were property owners, and they were determined that there should never be any persecution. The Committee would be very glad indeed to save any owner any unnecessary I expense. There wete cases in the list some of great hardship. One case was that of some property which belongs to a widow with only a life interest, and it was a: matter of extreme difficulty to know how to act in an equitable spirit. They weredesirious of acting in the most generous possible spirit, but they had also to look after the people who lived in the houses. The remarks of sCuogugneesitlelodr Bi ne a mh ls an,n ICiOnndeetrhnei n gq u theest ivo ni s tasat wofh ethcholerae ri t would be wise to employ some of the men at present un- employed through causes with which they had nothing to do, the men being out of work indirectly through the coal stoppage. It might be wise to employ some of them to clean out the back passages and areas which might be the means of preventing any outbreak of cholera in Warrington. Any expenditure just now in extra cleansing would be money well spent. Councillor SHAW drew attention to a number of the houses which it was said were unfit for human habitation particularly to Aikin-street, Back Aikin-street, Stam- ford-street, and Baxter's Yard. Ile would like to know whether cases were brought under the notice of the medical officer of health, or did he report them himself. The Chairman said that it wee the duty of the medical officer to report any dwelling which in his opinion was unfit for human habitation. Dr. Gornall was acting , according to instructions he had received from the Com- mittee. There was no desire on the part of the Commit- tee to injure the owners of property at all.—Councillor Slaraw wished to be informed whether Dr. Gornall visited , the property himself, or got a report brought to him by the person whom he had employed that the Corporation

SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.—PREVALENCS OF TRUANTING. The adoption of these minutes were proposed by Councillor Shaw Green. He said there was a large amount of truanting in the town,, and it seemed to him that the boys of the present day were less amenable to the discipline of their parents than when he was a boy.— Faeents were brought before the Committee who say " that they could not send their children to school. The Committee felt very strongly how important it would be to have a truant school, where the boys could be sent two or three months, and then return home. They had 24 boys in St. Anne's School,. Liverpool, which were costing the borough between £lBO and £2OO a year. There were 34 on the " Clio," who cost less than £5O. This showed how careful they had to be to keep down the cost, and showed the need of a truant school in connec- tion with other towns in this neighbourhood. They were very near the time when there would not be room for the boys and girls in the day schools now existing. There was an average attendance of 9,000,aud there was school accommodation for 11,800 children. Very soon more voluntary accommodation must be provided, or else they wonld be compelled to apply for a school board. Except for the cost he would rather like the latter, but he would not like the town to be at the expense • of building a board school. councillor PLUMFTON seconded the adoption of the minutes. He gave details of the visit of the deputation to the " Clio." He did not think the expense was thrown away. He thought that the subscription to it should be increased, as Father Nugent's school cost so much. Councillor GuesNiNetalso thought the subscription to the "Clio" should be increased. Councillor FLOOD said that the blame for the truant. ing was to a great extent due to the school managers. He knew of one school in particular where they fre- quently sent back the children because they had no school money. (Councillor Greening : "Name.") He thought that something should be doneby the Committee to enable children to attend school free until such times that pros- perity came to enable them to pay. Councillor GREENING. as a manager of a school ob- jected to the statement being made that children were sent back, unless-Abe name was also given. Councillor R. T: FAIRCLOUGLI thought that the magis- trates were not strict enough with the parents- when they came before them. Councillor HHA.TON ntxt spoke of the good appearance and character of the Warrington boys on the " COUBCiIIOT ROBERTS thought that the comparison between the cost of the children at Liverpool and at the "Clio" was hardly fair without a little explanation. The managers of the school at Liverpool made a charge per head of about 33.0 r 4s. per week, including maintenance, education, and clothing. If the managers of the "Clio" were not sufficiently business men to make a proper charge, he did not see why the committee should do it. With regard to Councillor Fairclough's complaint about the magistrates, Councillor Roberts saidithat unfortu- nately legislation would not allow the lads to be thrashed, and they got scot free. It was, out of the power of the magistrates to order their, chastisement, and the only course was to punish the parents. It went I very much against his desire to take out of the miser- able earnings of these people fines of 2s. 6d. and ss. They ought to Americanise the schools. They would have less trouble. Councillor SEAMAN was of opinion that the managers of schools were responsible for a good deal of the truant- ing. The CHAIRMAN said that the remarks of Councillor Faireough were hardly made out by the report of the In- spector, in which seven cases had been brought before the magistrates, fines being inflicted in six cases and one sent to the "Clio." He vevy much sympathised with Councillor Roberts. It wear very much against the grain to inflict any penalty upon the destitute people. Councillor SHAW GREEN said ehe committee did their best to persuade the children not to play truant, and help their parents. There was one school which had continually reported to them the names of children sent home because they had not got the fees. The committee did their best to look after the attendance, but there were only three officers. There were 9,000 on an aver- age, and to look properly alter this number they would at least have to double their officers. If they were to prevent the truanting they must have more power to deal with the children. If they had a truant sch.ool this would kill the insubordination of the children. If they could have compulsory free places in the sckools they would get out of the difficulty. The minutes were passed unanimously. MUSEUM, LIBRARY, ARTS, AND TECHNICAL INSTIMICTION

-TETE CEMETERY.

From the report of the registrar to the Cemetery Com- mittee, it appeared that the interments for the past five weeks were—adults, 39 ; minors, 109; stillborn and premature, 10. A BURY MAN'S EXPERIENCES.

(Bury "Guardian," July 29, 1893.)

Mr. Henry Filbey (as was ascertained by independent enquiry in the towri„ is a native of London, but came to reside in Bury about twenty-five years ago. He then enjoyed robust, radiant, physical health. Tall in stature and of fine physique, he enjoyed the blessings of a vig- orous constitution, until two years ago, when he com- menced to be troubled with excruciating pains in his head,, and an agonising sore throat. His appetite diminished bean alarming extent. Indeed, his throat got so bad that he almost choked at times, and it was consequently. impossible for him to take the little nourishment he did without considerable difficulty. For two months three ounces of food per day was all of which he partook. In the course of two years he lost considerably in weight,. and grew so weak that it was with the greatest difficulty he managed to get about at all. In fact, he was described as being " hardly able to orawl." If he proceeded the shert distance of about fifty yards he would use the use of his right leg and would have to stand for a time before any vitality would apparently return to it. Mr. Filbev is a well-known man in Elton, having lived at one address for the long period of a quarter of a century, and his numerous friends: noticed the terrible change which the insidious malady from which he was suffering had wrought in him. A\ doctor was called in, and he did what be could for the unferturrate man, who, however, obtained little or no relief, and hie, medical attendant said he could not do anything.more for him, but advised him to go to the Manchester Infirmary. Mr. Filbey got there after a great effort, and was carefully and thoroughly exam- ined by the doctor,who informed him that he could not do anything for him asan out-patient, nor could any other doctor,. It was during the interval elapsing between a first and second visit to the institution that Mr.Filbey had the good fortune to read in a Manchester evening paper of a remarkable cure effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Mr. Fahey decided to try these pills, and the result was like magic, so beneficial were they. Within three days he found relief. Renewed hopes now took the place of despair, and he continued to take this marvellous remedy with increasing benefit. He is-now an entirely different man, and in his own forcible man- ner he describes these pills as a" grand medicine." Having .learned these facts independently, our repor— ter with a view to verification, called personally upon Mr. Henry Filbey, who resides at 39, Baker-st., Elton. , That gentleman himself opened the door and gave our representative a cheery greeting. Mr. Filbey was so. enthusiastic in his praise of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills that it was almost unnecessary to ask a single question, and we give his statement as he himself gave it to us. "It is," be said, "two years since I, was first taken. illeand I was at home last year for five months,. and I have been at home again since the 3rd March of this year. The pains in my head were shocking, and I have been very nearly out of my mind. Indeed, I was so ill I was not expected to live. My throat was very bad,. and I have been almost choking. My doctor said there Was something in my throat, and the best thing I could do was to go to the Manchester Infirmary. I was vary ill and weak at the time, and I had a job to get there'. I, however, managed to do so, and the doctor examined me thoroughly. He agreed with my own medical ad- viser and said there was something in my throat, bathe could not possibly, he said, give maanything to do me any good, nor could any doctor that he knew of. That was, be could not do me any good as an out-patient. Ile told me I had better make up my mind to go inside, and he would find a bed for me. I was to go the following Wednesday. In the interval I picked up a newspaper, and saw a paragraph about a man who had met with an accident and had been in a hespital for some time. At last somebody recommended to him Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which he took, and he experienced a wonderful change in his health for the better. I said to my wife, 'I will try these pills,' and I accordingly sent to London for a hax, I took the pills and in three days felt better. The day before I was to go to the institution again, I said to my wile, 'I feel better and I think, L shall get well, but I will go and see what they say at the Infirmary.' So I went, but they had not a bed ready for me, and I eamaaway and told my wife I should keep on with the pills and would not go into the hospital. I continued to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and I took the contents- of four boxes and kept on improving. In fact, I came on won- derfully well. I had heceme so weak that I could not walk fifty yards without resting. The use went out of one of my legs, and 1.) had to wait five minutes before the use returned and I could go on again. I used to weigh thirteen stone two years ago, but I lost three stone weight. I arn now gaining ground gradieelly,and am a different man altogether to what I was a short time ago. Oh !.bless you; yes. If you had seen me a couple of months. ago you would not have given three halfpence for my life, and I don't think there is any- thing but Dr. Williams' Pink Pills that has set me to rights so far. I have recommended them to several people. It is a wonderful medicine ! I feel better now than I have done for a long time. 2 can now walk a mile without feeling the effect, and the pains have left my throat and I can eat very well again." Mr. Filbey's friends also testify to the trying illness he has endured. With a desire to learn the fullest particu- lars and rezeive confirmatory testimony our reporter visited a xeighbour who had known Mr. Filbey and his family for a great number of years. It was Mrs. Parker a lady residing at 11,Baker.st.,Elton, Bury, whose state- ment speaks for itself. She said "Mr. Filbey was very ill, and I thought lie would never do any good again. lie could hardly crawl up this street, as he went along with his hands on his head. I was very sorry, and used to think many a time he would break down. I thought he would never get better. He was quite deaf' but there is a wonderful change in him. The evidence of this marvellous cure is indisputable, and however tardy the public might be in accepting statements of popular medicines, there is no getting be- hind the fact that a wonderful cure has been effected in this case, which is another tribute to the efficacy of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. 'They enjoy—and deservedly so— a fame extending to all parts of the world, and with the greatest confidence we recommend them to the public generally. After the foregoing there can be no reasonable doubt that Dr.Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are a positive and unfailing cure for all diseases arising from an urn. poverished state of the blood, such as Anaemia, Pale and Sallow Complexion, Green Sickrrss, General Muscular Weakness,Depression of Spirits, Loss of Appetit,e,Palpita- tion of the Heart, Shortness of Breath, Pain in the Back Nervous Headache, Dizziners, Loss of Memory, Early Decay, all forms of Female Weakness, Hysteria, Paraly- sis, Locomotor Ataxy, Rheumatism and Sciatica ; also of all diseases arising from Vitiated Humours in the Blood, which cause Scrofula,Riekets.Hip-joint Diseases,Chronic Erysipelas, Consumption of the Bowels and Lungs, and that they invigorate the Blood and System when broken down by overwork, worry, diseases, excesses and indiscretion of living. These Pills are not a Purgative Medicine. They contain nothing that could injury the most delicate system. They aot directly on the blood, supplying to the latter its life-giving qualities, by assisting it to absorb oxygen, that great supporter of all organic life. In this ways the blood becoming" built up," and being supplied with its lacking constituents, becomes rich and red, nourishes the various organs, stimulating them to activity in the performance of their functions, and thus eliminating diseases from the system.

THE COAL TRADE CRISIS.

EFFECT ON THE L AND N.,W. COMPANY.

The London and North-Western Railway Company, who are suffering most severely not only from want of coal, but from diminished traffic have issued an order stating that goods inspectors, bralestnen and shanters in the running department will from Mbnday be put on short time. In certain districts the company are reduc- ing the number of their employes and curtailing by nearly one half the working hours of the remainder.

MEETING OF MINERS AT ST:. HELENS. THE BALLOT REFUSED..

• A meeting of miners was held in the Circus, North- road, Et. Helens, on Tuesday marning,the business being to consider the request of the Executive Committee of the Miners' Federation that a ballot should be taken on the questions of accepting the 2.sper, cent. reduction or some part thereof, of agreeing to arbitration, or of going to work where the masters are willing to withdraw their notice. Only bonafule employ& in collieries over eighteen years of age were admitted. About r,ogo persons were present. The men seemed in cheerful spirits, and laughed and joked good humouredly while waiting for the commencement of the prooeedings.—Mr. George Wilkinson was voted to the chairl and In opening the meeting said he hoped the battle would be fought as quietly, orderly and peaceably asit had been in the past five weeks. (Cheers.) Another thing ;,while they were in the battle, let them fight to the bitter end_ (Loud cheers.) —Mr. Thomas Glover then explained the wish of the Federation, and said the decision to take a ballot was on account of statements having been made that the leaders . of the men would not allow them. to arrange any terms with the masters. They, therefore, wished for the opinion of the men.—(A‘ voice :: "They have got it already.")—The men were unanimous in negativing the request to ballot on accepting the 25 per cent. or any reduction,.and as regarded the question of allowing any. one to go to work even at the old rate of wages only about a.doxen voted for it; and a resolution not to ballot on it was carried with cheers. A.resolution condemning the action of the guardians in refusing relief to miners and their families was unanimously carried, and it was ' decided to go in a body to the guardians' meeting on Thursday and ask how it was the guardians were not carrying out the !swathe oountry and relieving distress. Mr. Glover said he mist have a pledge that they would be orderly at the workhouse, and this was given. A letter had been received from the solicitors to the Cotham trustees stating that no meeting of miners must in future be held on Hardshaw Fields, unless permission was-dbtained from Mr. Campbell,,of Wigan. Mr. Glover said if the men were prepared to meet there on Thursday at twelve o'Clock he was also prepared,whether they were locked up or not. It was also agreed to meet the sug- gestion also made that miners, in pit garb, should join the trades demonstration at Liverpool on the 16th inst.

GENERAL PURPOSES. COMMITTEE.-THE OAS ANNUITIES.

At a meeting on the 28th August it was resolved that application be made to the Local Government Board for a provisional order to alter and amend the Warrington Corporation Gas Act. 1877, the Corporation Lighting and Improvement Act, 1879, and the Warrington Extension and Water Act, lam, so as to enable the Corporation to redeem the Warrington gas annuities by compulsory purchase at market price. The minutes were confirmed on the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Councillor SIIANT GREEN. The latter, referring to the question of securities, said they had no power to make the holders sell them. Practicallyl the debt remained the same after 15 years, and after having spent £24,000. It was time that they dealt with the minutes and put themselves in a position to compel the holders to sell them. 'Phe market price at present was 29 and 291. This concluded the business.

WESLEYAN METHODISM AT WARRINGTON.

RECEPTION hi ERTL:CO:

Last Sunday was the beginning of the Wesleyan Methodistic year, and more than 600 ministers dur- ing the past week have changed their spheres of labour from one circuit to another. There was a dt:uble change in Warrington, the• Rev. A. Freeman leaving Warrington for likely, and the Rev. T. Rosa for Queen-at. circuit, Hull. In their places, have come the Rev. Owen• Watkins, lately returned missionary from Africa, and the Rev. CIJ D. Mason,.l from Gloucester. On Sunday the pulpits of the 80ld-I st.,and Penketh Chapels were occupied by the Revs. o.Wakins and G.D.Mason respectively,and they had very good congregations at each place. On Monday night a reception meeting to the two new ministers was held in the Rold-st.,,sohoolroom..Previous to the meeting,. the ministers were introduced to the various members of the congregatian,and punctually at seven o'clock the meeting began. The chair was occu. pied by Mr. W. Silcock, senior circuit steward, who was, supported on the platform by the Revs. J, Kirtlan,.J, Watkins,.and G. D. Mason, and Messrs. E. Richardson It. Garnett, J.P., W. Bannett, E. Brown, J.: Summersgth and J. Taylor. The CHAIRMAN having very briefly opened the proceed- ings, the Rev. J. Kirtlan addressed the meeting. He said that it gave him great pleasure to be there that night. He was sure that the gathering would greatly stimulate the new ministers' and he hoped that they would have such a blessing that they would look back during the year with great thankfulness,. remembering that that night was the starting,point of a year of con- tinued success,and of triumphs allalong the line. Twelve months ago, he was in a similar- position as the new ministers, but he was glad to say that he felt very much more comfortable that night than he did then,, though he did not forget the great kindness wit h•whioh he was received when he came into that citcult. He should . . . .

like to say how glad he was to be • associated with the brethren who had come amongst them, brethren who had won their honours, and done the work in other spheres before they came to Warrington. He thought that two or three things could be improved in Warring- ton circuit, and as they had representatives present from almost all the societies in. the circuit,, he would take the opportunity of mentioning them. Their ser- vices would be very greatly improved in tone, if some of the members of the congregation would remember the time the services began. (Applause.) They could also improve considerably in their singing. lie thought I that the trustees of the chapel did not give sufficient , care and attention to the choirs, and he hoped that the members of the choirs would improve the regularity of ' their attendanoe: He also hoped that a little more at- i tention would be paid to strangers who came into the j chapels. A great deal was already done in this respect, but still there could be a little improvement. The 1 leaders of the classes should also endeavour to get more ' members in their classes. 110 mentioned theaepoints as i this was their first meeting of the whole circuit during ; the present year. The Rev. J. Gennexte, was the next speaker. He said that during the months of August, the demerits of the I three years system were made plain. He thought that I all present would have voted for an extension for an inde- finate period of .the services of the Rev. A. Freeman and j the Rev.T.Ross. During the present month, however,the merits of the three years system were manifested. But for this system they might never have had the two minis- 1 ters who nad taken their hearts by storm, and from j whom they expected very great things. He felt slue that their expectations-would not be disappointed. He did not hold a brief for the three years system, as he ' was more favourable to an extension of the term. ([Tear f hear),—and he thought they would have that before long. The Wesleyan Methodist had many peculiarities , but none more striking, especially to outsiders, tisan the marked rapidity and ease with which they extended their affections and confidence from one minister to ' another. This was a great mystery to the other churches They thought that relations between Methodists and their ministers was not of that strong, enduring charac- ter which makes the relationship between pastors and the people of other churches. Dr. Mackennal, ex- chairman of the Congregational Union, said that the relationship between the minister and the people in Methodism, was official not personal. He did not mean to des any injustice to Methodists, it would he far from his mind and intentions, but he did so nevertheless. He (Mr. Gunnell) maintained that there was no people more truly attached to their pastors as the Methodist people. (Applause.) Coming to the new 'ministers in Mr. Watkins, there would be something stimulating and instructive, and he thought that idlers, gamblers, and late comers would have a very bad time of it. If they had a specimen of the bulk of Mr. Mason, at Penketh on the previous „day,, he might always be sure of a hearty welcome whenever he appeared in the Penketh pulpit. In conclusion, he thought that the circuit had a good year before it.. They had a good and energetic superintendent free from all jealously and always ready to do his share of the work. The new minister would always be sure of the co-operation of the laymen, and they would find appreciative hearers wherever they went. The majority of the congregation he was glad Co say were young. This was a very good sign. He gave the two new ministers and their wives and families a very hearty welcome to Warrington, and he hoped that their time in the circuit would be a very prosperous one. The CHAD:MAN said that he would now call upon Mr. Watkins to address the meeting. Ile had laboured for more than 1 years in the Dark Continent. Mr. Wat- kins was not invited to the circuit, and ho could not be offended at the fight they made to get Mr. Carnson to Warrington. They had never doubted his capabilities as a preacher and platform speaker, and they were only afraid that by his pepularitv he would have so many calls that like John Wesley he would have the world as his parish. Mr. Watkins had however determined that during the three years he was in Warrington, he would work for and help to build up the circuit. In the name of the circuit he accorded Mr. Watkin3 a most hearty welcome. Mr. WATKINS, whose rising was the signal for loud and continued applause, in reply said that if this was the welcome they gave to the man they did not want, what- ever kind of a reception would they give if they had eat the man they did want? (Loud laughter.) He might tell them however, that if they had not fought for Mr. Carnson, he would not have thought very much of them, as they ought to stand by the man they had invited. He was glad to be in Warrington, as he liked hearty folks, and when people shook him properly by the hand he felt three parts at home if they opened their mouths. (Laughter.) He greatly enjoyed preaching the previous day in the grand old sanctuary at Bold-st., and also greatly enjoyed the singing. If they would succeed, they must have good Methodist singing. They must net have that high class, scientific singing in which the con- gregation could net join. They ought to pay more attention to congregational singing, as the Methodist revival was in a very great measure a revival of song. He had the heartiest sympathy with the remarks of the superintendent with respect to the choir. Ile did not believe in the Wesleyan ministers going on to political platforms, but there were subjects on which they should speak out such as the temperance question and other social subjects. The rev, gentleman finished a splendid speech by a magnificent peroration in which he urged upon everyone present to help on the work of God in the town. Mr. E. EnowNre then spoke representing Lymm, and Mr. R. Garnett spoke for Penketh. Miss BESSIE BUTTON hopod that the people would do everything to help the ministers in their work and not hinder them by neglect. She also welcomed the wives of the ministers. Mr. W. BENNETT having spoken for the Padgate Westeyans, the Rev. G. D. Meson was called upon. He expressed his appreciation of the hearty welcome which was accorded to him. He thoroughly endorsed Mr. Gunnell's remarks with respect to the extension of affec- tion from one minister to another by the Methodist people. He had been through the circuit and had ob- tained an insight into its working, and he hoped soon to know the people personally. 110 believed that the church should grapple with social problems, and he was glad that there was a vigorous temperance organisation in the town. Be hoped to take a part in the work before very long. He would also do his best to help on the musical part of the services. He hoped that the workers of the circuit would work together with the minister, and that they would have a very successful year to come. After a short address from Mr. J. Sunsmersgill from Sankey Bridges, the meeting came to an end with the singing of the Doxology and the benediction.—The pro- ceedings were interspered with a musical programme as follows: Pianoforte solo, Miss Pigot ; song "Anchored," ' Mr. Wallington ; song, Miss Gornall ; instrumental quar- tette, Miss Pigot, Miss Winni Pigot, Miss Bennett and Mr. Pigot ; anthem, "Seek ye the Lord," by the choir; violin solo, Mr. Boothroyd ; and song "Flight of ages," Miss Brown. There was an interval of half an hour, , during which refre3buints were provide,

ST: HELENS TRADE PARALYSED.

It is-said that the banks in St. Helens are turning over about £30,000 a week less than in ordinary times, and as the bulk of this would be paid out for wages a sure index to the state of trade is given. A tradesman on Wednesday morning remarked that there was " no- thing:doing," and he was- £2.01k worse off than he was a month ago. He was "giving," and had practically nothing coming in. This is the experience of many local tradesinen.

WHOLESALE TRAIN REDUCTIONS.

The Midland Railway Company have felt themselves conipolled, in consequence ot the state of the coal market, to economise, and besides having partially closed their extensive works at Derby,by which about 7,000 men lieve been effected,they have now decided to shorten their train service, and commencing on Monday next some thirty passenger trains will be discontinued. Still greatewpre- _cautions may become necessary unless the outlook im- proves.
