A STRANGE RUMOUR.

On Wednesday a rumour was pre{ alent in Day dock that the strike is to terminate at the end of next week, and the colliers there regard the news as autkantk. It is stated that certain Wigan coalowners told the men that they could prepare for work next week. It is alleged that the masters made a compact to, keep the pits closed for six weeks on pain of fine, .after which work could be resumed. DISTURBANCE IN WALES AND YORTZMIRE.

There was again a considerable amount of rioting at the colliery centres of Sentla Yorkshire and North Staffordshire on Wednesday. At one or two pits the re- maining stacks of coal were set on fire,„n,rui at the Wath Main Colliery, near Barnsley,damago.toAhe extent of several thousand pounds was done. At several collier- ies the police came into conflict with the.rolners, but the presence of the military In the principal areas of dis- turbance had the effect of reducing the violence of the rioters. As a result of meetings held :crt.. Wednesday, the strike in South Wales is reported tc be practically at an end. At Llanelly, where there weroserious disturb- ances on Monday and Tuesday, the presence of the mil- itary and an elan, force of police apiylearis to have re- stored order. Owing to the treatment they have received at the hands of the Welsh miners, many of the Scotch and Durham" blacklegs" have left the district.

COLLINS' GREEN..

As a result of the disturbances which took pace at Bold Colliery and Collins Green last week, .when a num- ber of day.wagemen were prevented from descending the pitsfa meeting of colliers was held at Burtonwood, on Friday afternoon, when Mr. T. Glover, J.P.', Miners agent, addressed the men. After going into the question a resolution was adopted to the effect" That no men le (snowed to•work, with the exception of furnace men, and men ta look alter the water." Several rumours were about that further disturbances were likely to take place at Bold Colliery, but so far, everything is peacc— able.

IMPORTANT DECLARATION BY DE2BYSIIIIIE',. COAL OWNE.M3:.

The Derbyshire colliery owners are seriously feeling the continuation of the strike, as it affects their ability to keeptle mines from being drowned out. On Thurs— day one. of the leading owners declared that unless he can get slack for the pumps from some other district he cannot continue the Icckout for more than another week Strenuous efforts are being madeto get fuel from else- where,. but at present without success. If the water once over-powers the pumps the. colliery would never be started, and £lOO,OOO damages would be sustainc,i; TIIE I'OSITION AT EARLESTOWN.

Apparently there is no eliange to record in the serious condition of affairs in..this district, except thatthe distress which was bad enough last week is evidently on the increase. The closing of the Sankey Sugar Works has greatly iutensißtd, mat- tens, and relief has now not only to be adminisw tened to the families of colliers, but to those who a consequence of the strike are unable- to per- form their daily task. The outlook cannot be said to be anything but mos.L. serious, and every day rather aggravates the matter than otherwise. The stock of coal at the Waggen Works is dailygrowing less', and unless there is an early settlement of the dispute in the coal trade, there will be no- alterna- tive but to close the works, thus throwing out of employment something like 1,000 hands. The 1. majority of the men are already only working four days per week,and from Friday the men in one o the shops will be further reduced by.only working three days a week. Ev,ery effort is being put forth to relieve the prevailing distress, and a committee has been elected to deal with the question, called the Earlestown anti- District Central. Relief Com- mittee. It represents all denominations, and the Rev. T. R. Matthewe has been appointed chairman, Mr. T., Martindale secretary, and Mr. E. Hulme, treasurer, and it coastnenced active operations in the distribution of relief-on Thursday limning at the Mission Hall. Not only are the residents of Earlestown relieved, but families from (Mins Green and Haydock are the recipients of the charity. Mrs. Haadyside has with. great generosity uadertaken to provide 1,50 women and children IA ith their tea at the Missionliall every day for a week, and it is hoped her exampleswiit be readily fol- lowed by others. A strong committee of lay helpers in connection with the Central .Iteliei Ilind has been got together, and armed with the necessary Qxedentials they are energeticanq 4vorking thetowa,in,clistricts br sub- scriptions, the* efforts being under the circumstances generously responded to. THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1893.

THE House of Lords are treating The Bill in the the Home Rule Bill with much Lords.

more respect than they were one time advised to do by the least discreet of their supporters. That advice was, however, given when it was fondly hoped in some quarters that the mere revelation of the terms of the Bill, or at all events the discussion that ensued upon the re- velation, would result in discrediting the Govern- ment and in disintegrating their supporters in Parliament. But much has happened since then. The Bill has been revealed in all its details; it has been debated with the utmost keenness, not to say bitterness, and at an unprecedented length, yet there have been no upheavals of popular in- dignation in the country, and the solid ranks of the supporters of the Government have shown no signs of that disintegration which was so confi- dently talked about. The Ministers have through- out the prolonged fight against a watchful and, at times, a not over-scrupulous Opposition, been backed up by majorities,which,though fluctuating from night to night as must always be the case in Parliamentary warfare, have displayed practically no indication of permanent diminution. With such a respectable endorsement upon it the Bill was, at the end of last week, consigned to the tender mercies of the Lords. Under the circum- stances, there could be no suggestion of giving it that contumelious treatment which was at one time hinted at, and even openly threatened. As the Bill had weathered the storm so successfully in the House of Commons, it would not have been judicious on the part of their Lordships to "throw it out" without even discussing it. It was, therefore, read a first time en Friday night, immediately after passing the third reading in the Commons, and on Tuesday evening a "full-dress" debate was opened upon it in the Gilded Chamber. That it would be rejected was,of course,beyond dis- pute, but it is, in its way, some little satisfaction to know that the Peers have felt called upon to offer an elaborate defence of the action they are taking. Had they been as certain as they profess to be that the country is on their side, the Bill would have received a short shrift indeed. The distinction of moving the rejection of the Bill was conferred on the Duke of DEVONSHIRE. The Duke, as everyone admits, is the possessor of many solid qualities, but among his acquirements is not that of lively oratory. It has been said that when, as Marquis of Hartington, he made his first speech in the House of Commons he attracted much attention by stopping to yawn in the middle of a sentence. It may be surmised, therefore, that at that time, though he did figure as a Liberal politican, he was not animated by any of the vehemence of the popular reformer. Nor has he been at any period of his career since then. Probably no political proposal of recent times has so ruffled the equanimity of his temper as that of Hoine Buie, yet it is reported by one eye-witness of the historic scene in the House of Lords on Tuesday evening, that at some points in the Duke's speech "the most pressing question in the minds of onlookers was whether the Duke would fall asleep first or whether precedence would be taken by the audience." What was wanted in fire and force in the indictment of Home Rule by the Doke of DEVONSHIRE, was, however, on the fol- lowing evening amply supplied by that of the Duke of ARGYLL. This descendant of Highland chieftains was particularly fierce in his denuncia- tions of Mr. GLADSTONE. The Prime Minister is, he tells us, a "fanatic," and the Bill he has con-;

ceived "an imposture." The Duke of Attaya i 5 clearly of the same opinion as another eminent Scotchman, that the inhabitants of these islar'ds are mostly fools. For the Duke cannot denoun°

the Premier without at the same time denouneini the people amongst whom he lives. Mr. GOP' STONE 13 supported by more than half the flotO of Commons, and by more than half of the peolb who elect the House of Commons. It was a great° man than the Duke of ARGYLL who said," You call int indict a nation."

THE Trades Union Con,gress--er The Labour Parliament. the Parliament of Labour as it hsl. been aptly termed—is this Wet in session at Belfast. The debates and decisions c" the three hundred and eighty delegates who are' present are of greater significanco than the pr°- ceedings in the House cf Lords. These delegate. iirectly repressnt close upon a million working men, while indirectly they speak on behalf of rusgi: more. The hereditary legislators,. represer" nobody but themselves.. While the Lords are as. reactionary as ever, the Trades Union Congress iS more and more outspoken every year in dealia with social questions. On Wednesday, Mr. 131: TILLETT moved a resolution in favour of indepeou' ent labour representation, and—as a necessari corollary—of the formation of a fund to meet 011 the incidental expenses. This in itself went littlfr if any, further than proposals which had beg favourably received at previous Congresses, but remarkable addition was made to it in the course of the debate. A London delegate moved en, resolution to the effect that " Candidates receivir) financial assistance must pledge themselves t°3 support the principle of collective ownership ag,, control of all the moans of production and distr) bution, and the labour programme as agreed uP°lii from time to time by the Congress." This, course, as was quickly pointed out, is rs!). Socialism. A State in which there is a " collet ownership and control of all the means c production" is, beyond question, a Socialisli State. As was remarked by a Durhsg) delegate when a proposal of this kind was put he' fore them it was necessary for the Congress to de- fine its position—whether it was to be "T 1 Unionist," or whether it was to be manipulated !Y Socialistic tendencies. The answer to this aPPeI was emphatic and decisive—so far as the preseotogr, Congress is concerned at all events. On g being taken the Socialistic proposal of the Leo :• - delegate was carried by the large majority hundred and fifty,to fifty-two votes. It is questl,eeil able, however, whether the Congress quite resits t the full meaning of the resolution it had passed; immediately after, it rejected a proposal of- ▪ „ KEIR HARDIE, M.P., that Labour members of t" House of Commons should always sit on the or position benches until they were numerous en'grlg.o to form a Labour Cabinet. Yet a party whose rse,lo plank is the collective ownership and control oft"_A means of production can scarcely be iderltl?: with either of the existing parties. Allowl.; however, for all deductions the resolutle seems to indicate a wide departure from to Traci policy of what is known as the "old ". Unionism.

How to deal with the 1113'.;1 The Unemployed. ployed is likely to be a creel' question during the coming Iglu; ter. It seems to be a general opinion that, eveli after the colliers have resumed work, there still be in all parts of the country an abnorrae',n) large number of men who will find it impossible v; '-obtain work for their willing hands to do. rrhije being so, great responsibilities will fall upon ptib'o, bodies, and particularly upon Boards of Guardiseo As a rule Guardians are difficult to move fe°.r their usual red-tape routine, and indeed the„if power of initiating new methods is not ve's ample. HARVEST THANKSGIVING AT PADGATE. On Thursday evening, the annual harvest festival was held at Christ Church, Padgate. There was n good congregation, and the church was tastefully decorated with flowers, vegetables, corn, and other farm produce, which gave the church a pretty appearance. The preacher was the Right Rev. Bishop Royston, D.D., assistant bishop of the diocese, whose sermon was suitable to the occasion. ALL HOUSEHOLD LINEN, hemmed and marked. free of charge.—J. and W. Durros. Sankev-st.—Advt.

- FARM FIRE. Information reached Warrington early on Afon- day morning, that the farm premises of Mr. Isaac Warburton, Woolston, were on fire. On the arrival lof the fire brigade it was found that the barn and tiiippons were completely destroyed as the fire had continued for a considerable time before it was dis- covered. However, all the live stock were safely rescued. The firemen directed their efforts to pre- venting the spread of the flames, and in this they were successful, although the supply of water was some distance away. The damages, which are covered by insurance, amount to .£3OO. The origin of the fire is attributed to the carelessness of a tramp. ALL HOUSEHOLD -LINEN, hemmed and marked, free of charm.. —3. and W. DUTTON, Sankey et.—Advt. AN AT'TEMPT TO BREAK The RECORD. Fletcher, the well-known cyclist, who hopes to break Mill's record in the journey from Land's End toJohn O'Groat's, arrived in Warrington on Wed- nesday morning at 9.18. He looked remarkably fresh and well. He was accompanied by Siddall and Adams. His appearance at this early hour was altogether unexpected, and he passed through the town almost unnoticed. He partook of refresh- ments at the Norton Arms Hotel, but only is seven minutes. Up to the present he s a lew minutes within Mill's record, but he has the greater part of the journey yet to perform. ALL HOUSEHOLD LINEN, hemmed and marked, free of charge.—L and W. Durrox, Saakey-st.—Adt.t.

ANNUAL TREAT. The annual treat of the scholars of the Croft Board School was held in splendid weather on Saturday afternoon. At, one o'clock the children assembled at the Board School, and at two a pro- cession was formed, and headed by the Firs-lane brass band they paraded the village and afterwards returned to the school for tea, to which about 250 persons sat down. Nuts and sweets were distri- buted, and the children proceeded to a field on Heath Farm (kindly lent by Mr. E. W. Sankey, one of the members of the School Board), where a variety of games and sports took place, and the band played for dancing until dusk. Dancing afterwards took place in the school until eleven o'clock. _ . .

----- UMBRELLAS RE-COVERED.—J. and W.DUTTON. Sankey-st., Warrington.— Adv. SHOCKING SUICIDE. A shocking suicide took place on Thursday at Warringoon. A labourer at Messrs. Crosfield's Soap Works, named James Unsworth, residing at 17, Bostock-staeet, had been rather de- pressed in spirits for some time, but followed his employment until Monday. He went to Dr. Jack on Tuesday and was supplied with some medicine. He was very nervous and appeared to think that some on was after him to do him some harm. Early on Thursday morning he went a walk with a friend named William Princep. They returned at about 8.30 a.m., and Princep left deceased at his own house. At about 11.15 deceased was in the kitchen when without any warning he picked up a razor and cut his throat and died soon afterwards.

WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY.

A preliminary meeting of the above named Society was held in St. Austin's Chambers on Thurs- day evening. The business of the meetitig was to elect officers and prepare the syllabus for the ensu- ing session. There was a good attendance and a good number of members were enrolled. The chair was occapied by Mr. Hulse. Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., was re-elected president and all the vice- presidents were re-elected. Mr. John Gandy was appointed chairmen of the committee, and Messrs. Kent, Hulse, Sdcock, and Longland were elected vice-chairman. The committee is to consist of the I following members :—Messrs. Horton, W. Cromp- ton, Elijah Ford, J. J. Neild, E. Lyon, and [Jarring- ton. Mr. Dutton was appointed treasurer, and Messrs. Arthur Bennett, and E. J. Hall, lion secre- taries. It was unanimously decided to hold the meetings in St. Austin's Chambers every Tuesday evening. At the first meeting to be held on the second Tuesday in October, Mr. Dutton was re- quested to open a debate on the " Action of the Ilouse of Lords with respect to Home Rule." Mr. A. H. Crosfield has kindly consented to take the chair. It was also decided to have a concert the first Thursday in October, the arrangement being left in the hands of Mr. Kent. A numberof papers were promised and the session promises to be one of the most successful the society has had. A vote of thanks to Mr. Hulse for presiding concluded the proceedings. NO REASONABLE OFFER REFUSED, if made at once at Msnmes Furniture Warehouse, Bridge Foot, Warrington, --See fuller Adv., page 4.—Adrt. SAD DROWNING .CASE. _ _

- --- An inquest was held at the Black Bear Inn,Latch- ford, on Wednesday afternoon, by Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy county coroner, touching the death of John Smith, a Ship Canal labourer, residing at Warrington View,Canal Bank,Latchford. The deceased was found drowned in the Old Quay Canal. near Black Bear Bridge, on Monday night. Mr. K. Smith, brother of the deceased, was the first witness called, and said deceased was 31 years of age. He last saw him alive on Saturday aPernoon, when he was leav- ing the " mail " about 2.30. Deceased was a very steady man and regular in his habits. -Richard Pilkington, of 571, Knutsford-road, said he was in company with the deceased on Satuiday night. He joined him about 9.30 in Walker's Vaults, Bridge- st.,and temained there with him until eleven o'clock when they returned home in comrany with another man named James Allen. They arrived at Black Bridge at 11.30 when they separated, the deceased going home along the canal bank. Ile considered deceased quite capable of walking home, though not perfectly sober. Constable Evans said from in- formation he received, he commenced dragging in the canal at 6-30 on Monday evening, and re- covered the body at 7.40. It was about 80 yards from the bridge and about 200 yards from deceased's home. The water was about 6ft. deep there. There seemed no signs of a struggle having taken place. The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned." Mr. Patterson, a juryman, suggested a rider being added, as to the unprotected path, but it was found !hit the road was a private one,

TIIE EXAMINER, SATITDAY, SITTEMBER 9, 1893.

RUNCORN IMPRON EMEN T bIONEIS

The monthly meeting of tl.is Board was held on Wednesday morning, at the. Town Hall, Runcorn. There were present —Messrs.S. Taylor (chairman), F. 111,4wa11, W. Fisher, 11. J. Chloe R. Abel. W. Ilaudley, J. W. Lightburn, James Dobson. W. Han- kinson, D. Bisbrown, T. Lea, %V. Leach, and E. 'Wynne-Iluraphreys ; F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (secretary), M. Barker (surveyor), and Dr. McDougall (medical officer of health).

{useful member, and the ratepayets a representative who had been a valuable servant to them for a long period. Mr. Bisbrown added a word of appreciation to the ser- vices rendered by Mr. Fisher.—The Chairman stated that he had worked with Mr. Fisher for a number of years on that Board and in committee, and from experi- ence he could say that ho had been of great use and advantage to the ratepayers of Runcorn. (Hear, hear). —Mr. Fisher expressed his sincere thanks for the kind expressions made regarding his services. He had per- formed his little duties amongst them to the best of hia ability, and did not expect to find the manner in which he had done so to be estimated so highly. Though he was leaving the Board he could amaze them that he would not cease to take an intert in the affars of the town. It might be that at some future time he would again offer his services to the ratepayers.—The minutes were confirmed. RE-APPOINTMENT OF NUISANCE INSPECTOR. - - .

- - The Local Government Board in sanctioning the re- appointment of Mr. Percival, as sanitary inspector, at a salary of £125 a year, expressed their satisfaction at being informed that the Runcorn Urban Sanita7 Autho- rity were making arrangement whereby the inspector of nuisances for the district would be relieved of the duty connected with the sale of nightsoil.

DISTRESS IN WARRINUTO.

OPENING OF A SOUP KITCHEN.

As was to be expected the prolongation of the coal strike and its couse quent serious effect upon local industries has caused great distress .in Warring- ton. The ironworkers have been the most seriously effected by the strike, and fel some weeks a large number of them have not dono a " stroke of work." This has told sadly upon their wives and families, who have felt the pinch of hunger. Fortunately, however, the weather has not been cold and the distress not so -marked as it otherwise would have been. On Saturday afternoon at a meeting of working-men, held on the' Fairground, when,the question as to the best means of relieving the distress in the town caused by loss of employment by the coal strike, was considered, a resolution was passed calling the attention of the authorities and clergy of the town to the distress, and asking for something to be done to relieve it. At the conclusion of the usual business at thc Police Court, on Monday, Mr. Diggle came for ward and handed a copy of the resolution passed on Satarday to the magistrates. Mr. niggle was followed by some representative working-men of the town. The result of a conversatiob with the magistrates was the formation of a distress committee, of which the magistrates pre- sent formed part, to receive subscriptions and open a soup kitchen. A meeting of the committee was held in the Drill Hall at 1 30, to take into consideration the best means of getting to work at once. Mr. F. Monks, J.P., presided, and there: were also present Mr. ,T. W. Hay- ward, J.P., Mr. S. Wright, J.P., Mr. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr. S. Wilson (deputy clerk), and the de- putation who attended the police court. A telegram in reply to one sent by the Town Clerk was received from the Mayor (Mr.E. Greenall), in reference to the utibr, tion of the balance of .£lO from last year's distress fund. The telegram read : "Yes certainly, by all means." With the offer of food fr-un Mr. Diggle ani subscriptions promised, the CM eittee at once set to work in provid- ing soup and 1re...1. At seven o'clock the Drill Hall pre- sented an unusual appearance. Men, women, and chil- dren Ir:eta to be seen flocking to the hall with cans, eartiaenware jars of every description, and even buckei for the ga atuitous food. A long single file were waiting their turn, and for some time the committee were kept busy ladling the soup out and distributing the half loaves of bread. -

The kitehen WAR acain ripened on Tuesday, and was continued each day until yesterday, when a nouce was ; posted on the entrance doers, intimatiog that no more ' food would be distributed after that day until Monday. The tickets for reljefluave been in the hands of the Com- mittee, and about 1,500 people have received food each day, costing on the average about £7 104. per day. Toe fond consisted of soap, bread and pitatoes. Etch da.y 500 loaves have been given away, and about 20 loads of potatoes have been distributed; One hundred pounds of mmt has been used,in the soup each day. The relief funds received up to yesterday have almost been ex- hausted, and after Monday the supply will be discon- tinued unless the funds receive a substantial addition. An appeal for help has been §ent to the clergy in the town by Mr. Wilson, hon. secretary, and it may be mentioned that half the collections realised in Wycliffe Church to- morrow will be devoted to the distress fund. A football match is to be played on the Wilderspool enclosure to- day (Saturday) when married footballers will meet the single. The proceeds will be given to the distress fund. To relieve some of the out-of-works the Corporation have engaged about forty or fifty men in cleansing yards, courts, passages, tv.., in different parts of the town. After an examination of their circumstances by Alderman Wright, J.P. and Mr. John Price, one of the members of the Workingmen's General Relief Committee, they engaged those most needing pecuniary assistance. A number of children are to be regaled with coffee and buns, to-morrow (Sunday) morning, but the expenses are not being defrayed by the general relief committee. A committee has been formed at Dallam and Bewsey forges to relieve workmen, some of whom have been very reluctant to apply at the Drill Hall. • MEETING OF WORKINGMEN.

The meeting held on Saturday afternoon in the Fair- ground was convened by Mr. Diggle. Several addresses were given by forgemen and others. The speeches, which were short, were delivered from a lorry which had. been drawn up near the wall at the further end of the ground. There was a very small attendance of the pub- lic.—Mr. Diggle said he had convened the meeting to raise funds for those men who had been thrown out of employment owing to the colliers' strike. Be had found by mixing with people that a large number of them were starving. He was anxious for a soup and bread kitchen to be opened. It was well-known that the great bulk of the shopkeepers could give nothing, and he wished, therefore, to appeal to the higher classes. A workingman said he had done five " turns " in five weeks. Recently he went to work, but was so weak, owing to the want of find, that after he had been em- ployed two hours he had to return home. He had a wife and five children to kesp. He received about 63. for a day's work. Another man said he had "done nothing" for three weeks. He had a family of six to look after. He had nothing coming in. He had been round "cadg- ing," and by this means obtained some potatoes, butter- milk, and bread and cheese. He had to pay 4s. a week for rent. Another man out of employment said he was the father of nine children, two of whom were at work, but their united wages did not amount to 6s. a week. Another working-man said he knew of several families in Bewsey-road who did not know where their next meal would come from. People could not ray their rent if they were out of work. Several other men spoke; one lof whom said he had only done five weeks' work in 22 weeks. His family numbered five. Now and again the neighbours had given him a crust of bread. Another said he had not wanted for bread yet, but the state of affairs at his house was bad. He had done little work during the past five weeks. An ironworker said there were men present who were "hard up" and in want of food, but people were "a little bit proud and dia not ike others to know they were poor." Fhey would "sooner suffer unto death rather than do it." After an address from Mr. Diggle upon the origin of the colliers' strike, it was resolved that that gentleman be asked to wait upon the Mayor to ask for his assistance on behalf of those men out of emvloyment in Warrington. It was further re- solved, "That we, ratepayers and others, do pledge our- selves to do .11 that lies in our power to call the attention of the authorities and clergy of the town to the great distress that is in our midst through the colliers' strike, and that something should be done to relieve the suffer- ing of the people at once."

TH HEALTII• OF THE TOWN.

The 44 deaths showed an aver- ac rate of 2+3 4, and exclusive of the inquest cases of. 24 per thousand of the estimated population. Of them 21 were infants und4r ,ne year of age, and three were infants over one and under two years of age.—Mr. Bark, r ctated that the number of deaths during the .a me period last year was 311.—Mr. Ilisbrown stated there was an idea in the town that there was a large number of cases of typhoid and slow fever, and he would like some authoritative statement as to whether there was any need for an uneasy feeling.—Dr. McDou- gall remarked that there was always fever prevalent at the present time of the year.

PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY FOR RUNCORN. The Health Committee reported the Minutes would be submitted relating to the drafts ot orders which the local Government proposed issuing, permanently constituting a port sanitary authority for the customs port of of Run- corn' and providing for the performance by the Liver- pool Pott Sanitary Authority of certain duties which would otherwise devolve npon the Runcorn Port Sanit- ary Authority.—Mr. Lightburn,in moving the confirma- tion of the minutes, stated that although cholera was making towards England, there was nothing to cause alarm in their district. The Port of Runcorn commenced I little above Ince and went as far as Warrington on the Cheshire side, and from Dungeon to Warrington on the Lancashire side. The upper reaches of the Mersey touched upon four riparian authorities. These were the Runcorn Sanitary Authority, the Runcorn Urban Authority, the Corporation of Widnes, and the Corpora- tion of Warrington. The Runcorn Commissioners took the precaution throe years ago to make an arrange- ment with Liverpool to deal with every cholera-stricken ship as if they belonged to Liverpool,and to charge them with the cost. So far they had no cases, and thereforei it had uot cost Runcorn one penny. Although they were geeerally termed a slow authority.they were the only one which had made such an arrangement,and theywere three years in advance of their neighbours. Upon their agree- ment, the Local Government Board had based their in- structions. An inquiry had been held, and the result was the reception of two documents, one constituting a joint Board and the second a new cholera regulation. The Port Sanitary Authority of Runcorn was to become' of a permanent character, and was intended to deal with all infectious diseases. It was proposed to consist of 13 members amid the representation was based chiefly on the amount of tonnage of vessels dealt with at the several ports. Runcorn Union was to have five members, Runcorn Commissioners four members, Widnes three, and Warrington one. The 13,,ard would have a medi- cal officer and a clerk as permanent staff. The members were elected frem the Corporations named on that Board, and any vacancy through death or otherwise would be filled by the authority which the member represented. The costs of any question arising between the riparian authorities would be settled by the arbitra- tion clauses of the Public Health Act, 1875 ; and any question as to finance by the Local Government Board. Any vessel coming to Runcorn with disease fa board would be intercepted at Liverpool, and dealtwitti as though it were a Liverpool ship, and the costs incurred would be chargeable upon the Joint Board. In dealing with that matter he had three resolutions to propose. One had respect to their representation. This was based upon the tonnage dealt with at their docks. In doing so, credit had been given to them for 77,000 tons per year. That was based on half a year's work. The docks had been closed for twelve months, and taking the two pre- vious half years, the average was 85,000 tons, or some 170,000 tons for the year. This being so they asked that their representation be increased. The order constituted three as a quorum. That was insufficient, and 'seeing that joint interests were involved, they asked that an alteration be made to five. The other proposition was that it should be made clear that they could recover from the owner of a ship any costs that might,be incurred in dealing with it at Liverpool. One weakness in their agreement with Liverpool was supposing that cholera was in embryo in Liverpool, and the ship passed inspec- tion, and got into Ruucorn docks, then they had no power to force the captain back to the mooring station at the Sloyne. That was pointed out, and the Local Government Board and they had inserted a clause to meet the case. The only increased cost would be the permanent staff. He thought that ought to be an impe- rial charge, because it was for the universal good of the people, who ought to pay for it. He begged to propose the resolution named for the reasons stated.— Mr. Humphreys seconded the proposal—Mr. Wiswall supported the proposal. Fortunately that Board had the wisdom three years ago to come to 'a n agreement whereby Liverpool dealt with cholera-stricken ships, and it was rather surprising that after all the information given to the inspectors who conducted the inquiry, they had failed to grasp the situation fully, and consequently they had not got a proper representation. The Runcorn dock was the entrance to the whole district. fle thought the Imperial Exchequer ought to pay the permanent ex- penses.—The motion was unanimously carried.

Mr. Wiswall .wished to know if the Board could do anything in matters like that which a day or two ago came under his observation. lie saw two cases of live geese which had come from Ireland, addressed to a Runcorn tradesman. The cases measured about seven feet long., and four to five feet broard, and were only six inches In depth. Some of the geese were dead, and : others only just alive. It was possible that the birds which were dead might be sold in the town. There ought to be some supervision over tbinns of that descrip- tion. There was not room for the geese to move, and some of them were suffocated.—Mr. Leach wished to know if there was any supervision over the animals brought to be killed at the slaughter houses. He had heard of instances in which the animals had been kept without food for several days before being killed. fie had thought of calling the attention of the offiers of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals to the matter.

BALTON PAROCIIIAL COMMITTEE.

This committee held a meeting on Monday evening, when it was resolved that the Rev.J.ffanby be appointed chairman for the year. A letter was submitted from the Local Government Board with regard to the estab- lishment of offensive trades, and it was resolved that the clerk write to the Local Government Board pointing out that in view of the almost certain development of the district by the opening of the Manchester Ship Canal the trades mentioned in the sections would in all proba- bility be or sought to be established, and unless proper precautions were taken, would result in creating serious nuisances, and that the authority ought to have conferred upon them such powers as would enable them to effectu- ally deal with them. It was decided that the Rev. J. Hanby, Messrs. J. Baguley, T. Wright, and Joseph Stokes, be a sub-committee to settle the bylaws for Halton. Mr. Brandreth reported that the Infectious Diseases Hospital Committee had resolved that a permanent hospital vas necessary, -and should be provided. The question had been before the Authority a long time, but the recent smallpox epidemic had brought it prominently before them. It had been decided to ask Mr. Dansey, the Local Government board inspector, to attend an early meeting to enable them to fix upon the bait site for the institution.—The minutes were confirmed, on the prepositioa of Mr. Brandreth, seconded by Mr. Aaron.

Two more cases of scarlet fever had occurred at Kingsley, add two at Batton, and the infection in the lattet town- ship was attributed to school.

DEPUTATION TO THE MAGISTRATES.

Mr. Diggle presented the following resolation passed at the meeting on Saturday, to the magis- trates at the Borough Court, on Monday :—" That we, ratepayers and others, do pledge ourselves to do all that lies in our power to call the attention of the autho- rities and clergy of the town to the great distress that is in our midst through the colliers' strike, and that something should be done to relieve the suffering of the people at once.—Alderman Harrison : Do you propose , anything P—Mr. Diggle : I think the Mayor should call a town's meeting to consider the matter.—The deputa- tion,a large one,was then introduced to the Bench.—The Chairman (Mr.F.Monks) : This is not a matter in which the magistrates have any voice, or can do anything. You are just as well able to call the attention of the clergy to the 'condition of .c.hings as we are. Other people are suffering. I know that manufacturers are suffering just as you are. It seems to me that the sooner the struggle comes to an end, the better it will be for all ; and in- stead of calling upon the clergy to relieve the distress, which is likely to be indefinite, you should call upon colliers to do something, either to submit the case to arbitration or to do something else. The whole matter rests with them.—Mr. Diggle: That is what we intend to do.—Alderman Harrison : Your practical course is to apply to the Mayor.—Mr. Diggle : We expected him to be here to-day.—Alderman Harrison : He is not well, and he is not at home. It may be a week or two before he returns. He is the authority to approach.—Mr. John Price: We come to ask him to call a town's meeting to relieve the poor people who are actually starving. Some- thing must be done at once.—Mr. Monks : Who are suffering except ironworkers ?—Mr. l'rice : The distress at Town's-end is something terrible. Some men have been out of work five weeks. One ironworker told me that he had sold his furniture to buy food for his wife and children, that he could hold out another week, and that then he must have food if he had to steal it.—Mr. Diggle: At the Florence Mills 250 people have been en- gaged. About three-fourths of them are females, many of them are single women, ad it is feared that some- thing that is not desirable will take place unless assis- tance is forthcoming. They have been out of work a month, and it is not intended to start them again until the strike is over.—Alderman Harrison : It is a very sad , state of things.—Mr. Price thought the Mayor should ' open a subscription list and a, soup kitchen.—Alderman Harrison: There are so many people affected that many ' cannot afford to subscribe.—Mr. Diggle : It is the higher classes we want to get at.—Mr. Monks : So far as we (Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co.) are concerned, we have met the difficulty by giving soup and bread to our own people.—Mr. Wright : We don't find all employers so generous.—Mr. Monks : Don't you think the wisest course to pursue is to give your attention to shortening the strike ?—Mr.Malkin : We are powerless in that way. —Mr. Monks: It is an injustice inflicted by one set of workmen upon, another set. There are men who have no regard to the affliction and poverty they bring on other people by means of an organisation—Mr.Price We have no power to interfere with what they are doing. What we have to do is to deal with the distress under our eyes. We -don't inter- fere with trades unionism at all at the present time.— Alderman Harrison: The Mayor is the legalized authority to call the town's meeting.—The Town Clerk : hi the absence of the Mayor the Deputy-Mayor is the proper person to approach.—Mr, Haley: Would it be possible to have the matter brought forward prominently at the meeting of the Town Council ?-7.111r. Monks : It is not a matter that affects the Town Council.—Alderman Harrison: The Town Council have nothing to do with it. —Mr. Diggle: People don't want to be pauperized. They don't want to go individually to apply for relief. I am afraid that if they do go they will go in a body to the relieving officer.—Mr. Price said Mr. Diggle would on the following day distribute a quantity of potatoes

amongst the poor.—The Dawn Clerk: We have a small .sum in hand from last year's distress fund, and I am sure the Mayor will hand it over to the committee. I will telegraph to him for permission:—Mr. Burch: I don't think there has been such dietress in Warrington for 20 years. and if it is not mot I would not like to be held responsible for the peace of the town.—Mr. Malkin said they would like to have some official names added to the committee to give it weight. They (the working- men) were willing to do the work if they could get gentlemen on the committee with influence.—Mr. Monks: If I can be of any use I shall be glad to help you. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Diggle : I am so satisfied that distress exists that I have provided 50 loaves and ten loads of potatoes. —Mr. Monks: Se far as we (the firm) are concerned we are quite willing to merge what we are Wing into what you- are doing. I think if you arrange with the Town Clerk for the balance he has named you can get to work at once. There are, I know, all the appliances you require at the Drill Hall:—The Town Clerk: I think I can promise the use of the Drill Hall. I think there is about £lO in hand from the last fusid.—Mr. Monks: 1 should advise the committee to get to work with the funds itlihand, and with the appliances which will be at yourbrisposal. There is no reason why you should not begin work to-day.—Mr. Malkin (to the Bench) : We are very pleased with your assistance and you co-operation. We libpe we shall be able to meet the poverty that is in our midst. We thank you very much.

_MEETING AT TUE DRILL HALL.

......... A meeting was held in the Drill Hall on Monday after- noon to miake arrangements for alleviating the distress. The Mayor was appointed !min treasurer, and Mr. Stan- ley Wilson hon. secretary, to the genrral relief commit- tee, which will consist of the following workingmen and others :—Mr. Monks, J.P., Alderman Harrison,.P.,Mr. J. W. Hayward, J.P., Mr. S. Wright, J.P., and Messrs. William Diggle, J. price, T. Malkin. W. Burton, W. Eg- eintori, T. McGuinness, Joseph Bunch, T. Cox, W. Thoinpson, J. Houghton, A. Hartley, T. Hughes, W. Yoden, Joseph Trinnan, Joseph Garrisx, J. Wareing, John ilayley, Henry l'aylor (Police Court missionary), Jo-epli Poole, T. Wareham. W. Hewitt. Joseph Oakes, Thomas Waterford, Thomas Dale, and B. Homer. Mr. NIONIES thought each firm Should be asked to open an independent soup kitchen, but the suggestion was not generally approved.—Mr. W. Ilunror: stated what was being done at the works with which lie was connected (mord:s,lla and CO, to alleviate the distress which existed by the 111011 having been thrown out of erriploy- ment. He remarked that he was very pleased to know that the ironworkers as a body ba.,l the discretion to know that the sliding scale was the best scale to pay wages by. If colliers would only consent to have their wages governed by the sliding scale it would be much better for them and the country. Everyone who had been in an ironworks for a number of years Jtnew that it was morally impossible to force wages up or even to retain them in a declining trade. It had been tried years ago, and had proved to be a failure. They had adopted the sliding scale now, and they had no trouble with their men. Mr. 13Lietnn concluded by observing that he was very sorry for the ironworkers, who had gone through hard times. Since the American Tariff Bill passed, two-thirds of the inen had nothing to do.—Mr. PRICE announced that the surplus from last year's distress fund, £9 les., had been given by the Mayor to the present fund. That spoke well for the Mayor. The town Clerk has also contributed a guinea, without being asked to do so, and that showed his sym- pathy with the poor. Ten shillings and sixpence had been received from the proprietors of the" Echo." (Ap- plause.) He concluded by proposing a vote of,thanks to the Adjutant of the Volunteers for allowing the use of the room and to the magistrates for their assistance in the present crisis.—Mr. MALKIN seconded the motion, which was carried. He also proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and to Mr. Wilson for acting as hen. secretary to the Relief Committee.—This was seconded by Mr. Ilgisny Tame (P.C.M.), who said the Relief Committee had undertaken a very onerous and ardanus task. No one could' assist them better than the working- men now present. It was a fact that there were men who did not care about work. There was some impusi- tima practised at the previous soup kitchen held at Christmas, and he hoped, therefore, that the working- men present would help the committee in this respect, and thus make their task lighter. (Hear, hear.)—The motion was heartily agreed to, and Mr. Burton having expressed a hope that the strike would not last long,the committee dispersed. SPECIAL MEETING OF THE RELIEF COMMITTEE.

A special meeting of this Committee was held in the Council Chamber of the Town Hall on Thursday after- noon. when the Rev. John Yonge, who with Mr. S. B. Stringer was elected on the committee, occupied the chair. Mr. Stanley Wilson, hon. sec., said that he had received no subscriptions since Monday. The cash in hand was £l3 193. 10cl., and during the tour days of the week £23 14s. 2d. had been spent. There was sufficient cash in hand to carry on until the end of the present week. The total amount promised was £4O Is. 10d. This was made up as follows : balance from last year £9 163. 81, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle £1 Is., Mr. E. J. Guest Li, Mr. William Bolton Li, Mr. Roberts collected £7 113.81," Echo" proprietors per Mr.C.Lyon, 10s.8d.,Mr. T. Morris, £lO, Warrington Slate Company, per Mr. H. Roberts £5, Mr. W. Roberta collected £4 is. 6d. The Chairman asked if there had been any regular collectors. Mr. Malkin replied that there were not, but the meeting could appoint collectors.—Mr. Dale said that it would be much better if works such as Whitecross and Dalian) would do something like Messrs. Monks, Ball and Co. were doing.—Mr. Malkin said that he waited upon Mr. Morris and mentioned that to him, but he did not seem to fall in with tlio idea. He said it was a selfish idea as it only took in the people who worked at the works, but as the distress was general the funds should be general, and every one should benefit by it. There was a statement abroad with regard to Mr. Morris, that while the deputation prevailed upon him to give £lO to the fund, they had some diffi- culty in the matter, but he (Mr. Malkin) could say that Mr. Morris gave the money without any soliciting and also promised a further donation.—The Chairman was of opinion that Mr. Morris was quite right.—Mr. Burton said that Monks, Hall and Co. had had a soup kitchen nearly a week previous to Monday last. Considering that a large part of their men were out of the borough in Sankey Bridges, it would be bAter for them to be separate from the general fund and to have a soup kitchen of their own. They had distributed about 72 four lb. loaves that day. Considerieg all things it would be unfortunate if they Joined the general soup kitchen. He was sorry that Mr. Morris had said it was a selfish action on their part. He thought it was the reverse.— Mr. Malkin said that Mr. Morris did not refer to Monks, Hall and Co. Ile (Mr. Malkin) thought, however, that the employes of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. had a double advantage in receiving relief from the firm and being able also to come to the central soup kitchen as well.—Mr. Burton hoped that if any of his men were found getting soup they would be sent away.—Mr. Diggle complained that he had had no tickets for distri- bution,and that some people in Howley had not had any. Be was so bothered with people that he gave 72 loaves away on his own account in Howley.—Mr. Taylor said that tickets were supplied by Mr. Price who gave them to workmen to distribute in the works, and there were persons appointed to distribute them among those not connected with the larger works.—Mr. Bunch said that Mr. Diggle had no right in the room. (Mr. Diggle: How's that ?) He was not straightforward, and it be- came the duty of the committee to turn him off alto- gether. Mr. Diggle here interrupted, but Mr. Bunch told him that he would have to say something unplea- sant about bins if he did not keep quiet.—Mr. Wilson said that the question for the meeting was what steps should be taken as the fusds were rather low,and some- thing should be done to raise them.—The Chairman asked whether the funds were being distributed among the chronic poor. fie understood that they were for those thrown nut of work by the colliers' strike only.— Mr. Taylor said that it was a difficult thing to distin- guish them, but lie had refused several of the class Mr. Yonge named, when they applied. A number of the poor had had to be turned away that afternoon, as the soup, &c., was all given, but those persons could come next day.—Mr. Wilson suggested a town's meeting as the best way of getting over the difficulty, and a motion to bold one at 7.30 p.m.on Tuesday was carried on the mo- tion of Mr. Taylor,seconded by Mr.Dale.—lt was also de- cided that Mr. Wilson should write to the different minis- ters in the town, asking them to hold collections in their places of worship as soon as possible.—Mr. Wilson an- nounced that on Monday 760 persons hail been fed, on Tuesday 2.104, and on Wednesday 2.600.—Mr. Price said that on Sunday morning a breakfast of coffee and buns would be given to children. The meeting closed with the usual votes of thanks.

SANKEY BRIDGES. The distress at Sankey Bridges and the district round Mill-lane has been very acute. The inhabitants in these parts of Warrington are, for the greater part,ironworkers employed by Monks, Hall and Co., which for a fortnight has been almost completely closed. Messrs Monks, Hall and Co. have done good work by distributing soup, but still this has been scarcely sufficient to cope with the distress. The mem 'Dere of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel, deeming that it was 'part of their duty to help to relieve the distress about them, formed a committee on Sunday to collect subscriptions, and to di•tribute tickets for free teas during the coming week. They were so far successful that on Monday they were able to give a tea to over No children. On the following day, another tea was given to the men who were out of employment, at which 200 sat down. After the tea, a miscellaneous entertainment was given, Mr. Summersgill occupying the chair. The procee3ings were evidently enjeyed, to judge from the frequent demonstrations of applause. On Wednesday, a tea was given to the wives of the unemployed. The scene was a very animated one as the majority of the women had a child in arms. They sat down to a substantial tea after which an entertainment was given, Mr. Summersgill auain taking the chair. On the platform were Messrs. W. Brough. J. L. George and R. liighley, Miss Bessie Dutton and Mrs. Cartwright, of Moore, and Mrs. Watkins. After opening with singing and prayer, the Chairman expressed his pleasure at being at the meeting and seeing those present enjoy their tea. The people belonging to the place of worship had been full of sympathy for the distress in Sankey Bridge and he believed that this was only the beginning of what ' was to follow in trying to lift up the condition of those around. He did not say that they desired to have the

tables always provided. This cloud would pass away. They wanted to improve the social conditions of the people, and bring them more in Ouch with the gospel of Christ. —A song was now excellently given entitled " Ehren on the Rhine," by Miss Cannon, the audience joining in the - chorus.-7 Miss Bessie Dutton was next called upon and it was some time before she could make hen& heard owing to the continued applause with which she was received. She expressed her hearty sympathy with the poor people at the present time, and said hew glad she was that a Christian Church had taken this work inhand. She urged the women to take their troubles to the right source. She finished her address by I recitation entitled tt Which shall it be ?" which she gave in a very effective i marines—Mr. R. Eetwistle followed with a song " Over the bounding wave," in which the audience joined in the, cliorus.—Mr. Roylance, of Moore, gave an enthusiastic address and was followed by Mr. D. Griffith, one of the audience, who sang two songs in a very vigorous style and was heartily applauded. Mies Bessie Dutton next gave a reading "Mr. J. Cole," after which Mr. B. Mar- tin rendered a song and Miss Hotter a recitation.—The Rev. G. D. Mason said he was deeply concerned over the distress which he found in coming to Warrington. Be was of opinion that the church should not only look after the moral conditions of the people, but also their material conditions. When in Gloucester, at a meeting which Ben Tillett, Tom Mann, and other Labour leaders were present, he proposed a resolution which depre- cated -strikes. It was a thousand pities that strikes had to be resorted to when disputes occurred between mas- ters and men, and he wished some other method could be discovered by which they could be dispensed with, and thus not entail so much suffering. He was very glad that it had been put into the hearts of Christian men and women of that conaregation to try to dc some- thing to help their neighbours. It would not be enough simply to preach to them, they must do something for their bodies as well as their souls. There were no doubt a great many present who had never been in that room before, but they would all have a welcome whenever they came —Mr. Brough said that the Committee had had a great deal of work to do, as besides providing tea they had to get the money to provide it. He was glad to say that in almost every case when he had solicited subscriptions they had been helped willingly. After the hard work of that and the past few days it was advis- able to have a little rest so that at the close of the meet- ing each mother would have some food for the Thursday snd Friday given to her. The expense of these teas had been amply made out, and they had still some money in hand. It was therefore resolved to have another tea for men on Saturday, sod an entertainment to Follow. (Ap- plause.) lie would conclude by proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Summersgill and Miss Dutton for their presence that evening. Mr. Martin seconded and the motion was enthusiastically carried.—The motion was suitably responded to, and the meeting closed with the benediction. After the meeting, a loaf and some tea and sugar was given to each woman. It is very satis- factory to know that the subscriptions have been ample to cover the expenses of to-day (Saturday). -•- -

THE SITUATION AT THE WORKS.

There is no improvement to record in the position of the large works in the town effected by the coal strike. Each day the colliers remain on strike makes the outlook more serious, and should the struggle be continued much. longer, the distress which at present prevails will be con- siderably aggravated. 'Portions of the works at Dallam and Bewsey forges have been working during the week, but it is probable the whole of the works will again be closed neat week, or at the most one or two mills will be working. The whole of the works of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., with the exception of one mill have been closed during the week. A soup kitchen has been opened by the employers for the benefit of their workmen and families, which has kept them from starvation. At Whitecross works the mills have been working during the week. At Cockhedge Mills the coal which was stocked has been used up, and the supplies now obtained are, needless to say, costing the firm a considerable amount of extra expense. The firm are making this sacrifice to keep their employees at work, but it cannot be continued longer than a fortnight, when, should the zolliers be still out, the mills will be entirely closed. This will effect about 1,000 workpeople. Messrs. Rylands, Bros. Works are as yet unaffected by the strike and the same state of affairs exists at the Corporation Gas Works, their stock being sufficient for some time. At Messrs. Houghton's Sankey Wire Mills, work was partially stopped all last week, but on Monday they comincinced in full swing again. This has, however, only been brought about by the purchase of coal ab enormously enhanced prices, and the firm only hope to hold out a few weeks. As long as orders come in they' intend to keep the works going. Notices are posted upon the Cheshire Lines stations that after September 12, upwards of 20 trains will be stopped until further notice.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. On Tuesday afternoon, the fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at the Workhouse, Dutton,when there were present—Mr. J. Mclntyre (chairman),the Revs. J. W.N. Tanner and J. Hanby ; Messrs. S. Tay- lor, T. Lea, J. D. Jones, T. Handley, S. Yarwood, J. Knowles, J. Brandreth; E. Aaron, 11. E. Heywood, J. M. Williams, J. Parkinson, S. Woodward, C. T. The Rev. J. W. N. Tanner moved the appointment of a committee to visit the pauper lunati.is in the Chester Asylum chargeable to the Union, to consist of Messrs. Heywood and Parkinson, the Rev. J. W. N. Tanner,and the Clerk.—The proposal was seconded and carried.

THE H-ALF-YEARLY ESTIMATE.

Mr. Brandreili-sa-initted a report and estimate of the probable balance in favour of or against each township in the Runcorn Poor Law Union, at the end of the current half-year ending Michaelmas, 1693, and amount of con- tributions required from the overseers of the respective townships,to meet the expenditure of the half year ending Lady-day, 1894. This stated that the total capital for the current half-year amounted to £9,996, and the net prob.- able expend iiure was estimated at £7.200, which showed a balance at Michaelmas of £2,796. The probable expenditure during the enming half-year ending Lady Day, 1894, as set out in the detailed estimates, was— separate charges. £52; common charges, £2,000; county rate, £3,403; and balance and contingency fund, £3,732, total, £9,187. After deducting the probable balance in favour Of the townships at Michaelmas, 1893, the amount required by contribution orders from the overseers was £6,391. The rateable value of the Union, as per valua- tion lists in force, was £223,944, a decrease of £1,518 compared with the corresponding period of last year. A rate of 73. in the £ will produce £6,532, and it was recommended that a call of £6,391 be made upon the overseers, which was the same as for the corresponding period of last year. In proposing the adoption of the report, Mr. Brandreth stated that with regard to the county rate he saw some Guardians had been sign- ing a petition that the County Council should collect their sum direct from the overseers, for their present system made the poor rate to look such a large item. They only required £2,053, whereas the county received £3,403. He thought it would he a good thing for their Board to support the proposal.—Mr. Baxter asked what was the cause of the assessments being reduced.—The Clerk thought it was to a certain extent attributable to the Ship Canal as- sessments at Frodsham having been reduced.— Mr. Lewis I stated that landlords had reduced the rents, and the overseers had to follow.—The Clerk said the Liverpool water mains were formerly assessed at their full value' but the amount had been greatly reduced.—Mr. Lea seconded the motion, which was carried. RESIGNATION OF THE NURSE. ' " .

A letter was read from Elizabeth Williams, the nurse at the Workhouse, resigning her position. In accordance with the recommendation of the House Committee, the resignation was accepted, and it was decided that it take immediate effect. It was resolved that the master tem- porarily engage a trained nurse.

THE SHIP CANAL.

-- ANOTHER ARBITRITION : UMPIRE'S AWARD. The umpire (Mr. Fenwick, C.E, of Leeds), whir recently sat in Manchester to assess ihecornpensation to he paid by the Manchester Ship Canal Company to the Midland and Manchester, Sheffield, and Lincolnshire Railway companies as owners of land requird tor the purposes of the Canal, has issued his reward. The land in question comprised about 41 acres, and is situate in Manchester between the Bridgewa,ter Canal and the river Irwell. The witnesses for the railway companies valued the lands at sums varying from £38,000 to £40,000, whilst tlie estimate placed upon it by the wit- nesses for the Cana! Company was just over £16,000. The umpire has awarded £22,773.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALLEGED EMBEZZLEMENT. Bernard Purcell was charged bet ire the Warrington I magistrates on Saturday with feloniously stealing two cashmere pieces valued 205., on July 7th.—Mr. Browne who prosecuted on behalf of George Isaace said that his client carried on business at St. James'-st., Latchford. The prisoner was employed by him. The charge against prisoner was embezzling the money fir the pieces of cloth. A considerable amount of money and clothing were also missing.—George Isaacs who was called said that he had looked through prisoner's books, and did not find the pieces booked. There was also £2I less pri- soner's wages wrong, and three sets of clothes.— Catherine Logan, 29 New-road, at whose house the pri- soner lodged gave evidence to seeing the missing articles, after which Mr. Browne applied for a remand until Friday which was granted.—Mr. Jenkins applied for bail which however was disallowed. Purcell was again brought before the magistratesl yesterday, when in addition to the charge against him I en Saturday he was further charged wish embezzling I and stealing three suits of clothes on June 29th, ss. on ! August Ist, and al. on August 9th, also the property of George Isaac.—Mr. Browne repeated his statement of last Saturday, and also said that his client missed two flock beds which he had sent to the defendant, and came to Warrington to ask defendant where he had sold them. Defendant took him to a cottage in Latchford, and Mrs. Marchment, who resided there, said that she had paid 73. in sums of 2s. and ss. to the defendant,and produced the bill, on which was Purcell's own name. Purcell also admitted having the money. A suit of clothes was also missing which had been sent to him. In looking through the books several sums were missing, the total deficiencies coming to £24.—The complainant George Isaac was then put into the witness box, hut it was almost an utter impossibility to understand what he said. Be would start off at a tremendous rate, and keep at it until he was pulled up by Mr. Browne or the magistrates. What was unclerstecd, was confirmatory of Mr. Browne's statement. In cross- examination he denied that he entered part- nership with defendant. He received £5 from defendant as security but not to enter partnership with him. He paid 15s. per week and is. per Li commission. —Mrs. F'oggan gave evidence to seeing the cashmere cloth and the suit of clothes coming to her house in St. James-at., at which the business was carried en. In answer to Mr. Jenkins she said that there was no con- cealment in his actions.—Robert Foggan and Mrs.March- mont were also called for the prosecution.—Mr.Jenkins for the defence, maintained that his client was a part- ner with Isaac. There was no evidence of criminality, and if his client were brought up at the County Court far the goods,they had a complete answer to the charge. —Make. Nicholson (one of the magistrates) said there was no evidence of criminality in defendant's conduct. He did not think that the two parties had beer part- ners, and did net think there was any intentional steal- ing. It was a very common thing for travellers to deal in their own names. He thought that as defendant went apparently willingly to Mrs. Marchment's house that disposed of the question of criminality. As to the cashmere dress and suits of clothes, he might have held them for the security. There were some very loose trans- actions bet seen this class of men.—Peter Green and Thomas Morris were called by Mr. Jenkins, and each Swore that they had been present when the complainant and defenc'ant entered into partnership.—The Bench dis- missed the case. A DUCKING PREFERRED TO BEING ARRESTED. At the Warrington Borough Court on Saturday, Edward Scott appeared to answer a charge of being drunk and disorderly in Mersey-st. at 8.30 the previous night. This prisoner was evidently determined on weeping capture on the previous night. He was rather the worse for drink and had called at a shop to beg some bread and on being refused snatched up half a loaf and made off. P.C. Roper was informed of the circum- stances and went after him. When prisoner saw the officer coming he ran to the river bank, and jumped into the river saying that he would drown himself. He was a capital swimmer however and kept in the river three- quarters of an hour. A large crowd of people assembled to see this unusual occurrence. After he had been in the river for the above mentiQned time he came near the side and P.C. Marsh who had come upon the scene, caught hold of him. Prisoner did his best to pull the constable into the river, but he was eventually pulled -out and marched to the police station. When he was brought before the magistrates the next morning, he was attired in a number of flannels, as his clothes were hanging before the fire and were still too wet to be put on. —The magistrates as a reward for his swimming, exhi- bition decided to find him lodgings at H. M.'s expanse for 14 days.

AN EXPENSIVE ABSCONDER. . At th Warrington Borough Police Court on Monday, Alfred Wood was charged with absconding on the 14th March, 1889, and leaving his children chargeable to the Warrington Union. Mr. Shearer, relieving officer, said he was called to a house in Orford-street on the date named, and found two children, aged six and four, absolutely destitute. He sent them to to the workhouse. A warrant was issued for the prisoner's arrest, but nothing was heard of him until Sunday,when he was ap- prehended in Glasgow. The children had been maintained 44 years, and had cost £lO4 for maintenance, and it had cost £5 18s. to bring the prisoner to Warrington. The prisoner had had nobody to maintain since he absconded. Be was sent to gaol for three months' hard labour. IMPORTANT TO SHOPKEEPERS.

At the Wallington Borough Court, on Monday,William Cook, hawker. Crossley-street, was charged with having between the Ist and 30th Juno, unlawfully sold unjust scales. Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk) prosecuted. Mr. Whittle said from time to time scales had been sent to the Weights and Measures Office with balances made of soft material, to be stamped. Mr. Place rejected them and returned them to the owners and told them to buy new ones as they did not comply with the Act of Parliament. It was found that these condemnod scales bad come jot) use again. The defendant bad been to shopkeepers and bought their old scales and re-sold them. Mr. Place, market inspector, said en the 24th of August be found two pairs of scales in the possession of Mr. John Sampson, School Brow, which were unstamped and had been condemn d. They were seized. Sampson pur- chased the scales from the defendant, Cook. He (Mr. Place) went to the defendant and asked him where he got them from. It was ascertained that he had bought them from a shopkeeper named Mrs. Davies, whose scales had been previously condemned. Mrs. Davies told the defendant when be bought them that they were condemned. The defendant had been to other shop- keepers purchasing their condemned scales. He gave Mrs. Davies two shillings for her scales, if they had been correct they would cost 15s. Martha Davies, shopkeeper deposed to selling the scales to the defendant. Mr. Whittle said the seller of the scales was liable to prosecution. John SampSon, 23, School Brow, said he bought two pairs of scales of the defendant for three shillings, but he bought them as unstamped scales. Mr. Place said the law was at fault in not giving the authorities power to destroy condemned scales. Defendant was }lned 20s. and costs. —Samuel Dodd, 11, Park Place, was charged with caus- ing te be sold unjust scales. Mr. Place said a pair of scales were brought to the sffice to be stamped on the 7th August by Mrs. Clough, Heath Side. As they had previously been rejected, from inquiries he found that the defendant had sold them to her. The woman went to the defendant and asked for her money back which he.gavalyr,.., He afterwards put them in a sale in the market,' whim they were bought by Mrs. Tomkins. 150 endalt was fined 5.5. and costs, 183. GI in all.—John

Sampson, 2.3, School Brow was charged with being in possession of two unjust scales. As the defend int had given evidence for the prosecution in one of the cases, he was dismissed on payment of coats. AN EXPLOSIV MAGAZINE FOR RIXTON. At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednes- day, Mr. W. 11. J. Jenkins appeared on behalf of the Carbonite Syndicate Limited, 39, Ampthill Square, Regents Park, London, for the magistrates permission k to erect a magazine for the sale of explosives at Rixton. An objection had been sent in by a resident named Bradbury, of Farm Cottage, Glaz-brook. The objection contended that the proposed magazine weuld be too near his property, and too near the Salvation Temple. Mr. Jenkins said the object of placing the magazine there, was for the sale of explosives for the completion of the Ship Canal, and for mining purposes. By the erection of a mound, the magazine would he within the area speci- fied by Act of Parliament. They had got permission from the Home Secretary and now asked the magistrates' consent. Evidence was given by Mr. R. Lonie, mining agent and Inspector Kelly as to the distances from buildings in the vicinity. The application was granted on condition the mound was constructed accordiao to plans submitted. • •

A WILTSHIRE LABOURER'S BILL OF

FARE.

The "Red Van" of the Land Restoration League has been visiting the villages and hamlets in Wiltshire dur- ing the past few weeks, and the lecturers have lent a helping hand to the Wilts General and Agricultural Workers' Union. This week the van has been in West Wilts. Sneaking at a meeting at Kingston Deverill the other evening, the lecturer, Mr. Riley. said a labourer's wife in East Wilts had supplied him with details cf how she managed to keep the family on Ils, a week. Her husband was a cowman, receiving that sum as wages, and their family numbered seven—five children, whose ages raged from three to twelve. This is how she spent the 119. :—Six gallons of brEad at 93. a gallon, 43. 6i. ; half-gallon of flour for puddings, qd.; flti. tea, 10d. ; lilb. bacon, Is. ; 11b. butter and cheese, 2s. Id.; 31b. sugar, Md. ; oil, 3d. ; half-hundredweight of coal, 6d. ; soda, id. ; soap. 3d. ; tobacco, 3d. ; total, 103. 10i4d. Nothing was allowed for clothing or shoes, and the woman told him that when they wanted those necessaries they had to reduce their food supply, or go in debt and get out as best they could. Except at Whitsuntide they never had any fresh meat. The position of that family was that of hundreds in the county of Wilts, and many had not more than 108. a week.

HOBBS THE CONVICT AND BILFOUIt THE EXILE.

James W. Hobbs, of Liberator fame, who is now un- dergoing twelve years' penal servitude in her Majesty's prison at Wormwood Scrubbs, is no longer a magistrate for the borough of Croydon, his name having been re- moved from the Commission of the Peace by the Lord Chancellor. The building in which the magisterial business is now transacted at Croydon was purchased from Newman, one of the Liberator convicts by J. S. Balfour, at the time when Jabez was contesting the borough against Mr. (now Sir William) Grantham, and for some years did duty as the head-quarters of the local Liberal Association, Lord Herscheli being president. When Balfour disappeared the Corporation were actually in treaty with him fnr the purchase of the building, and had he stayed in England a few days longer he would undoubtedly have received a cheque for a substantial um which, instead, went te the ofliial receiver.
