RUGBY.

WARRINGTON V. MOSSLEL—This match was played at Mossley. The Warringtonians were unfortunate in hav- ing to make the journey minus the services of one of their three-quarters, Barnes failing to join his companions at the Central Station. This necessitated a reconstruc- tion of the team and Jolley was drafted from the forwards, and J. Lawless filled the breach in the front rank. The Mossley team was as selected, and punctual- ity, was observed in this the first match. Walker kick- ing off up the incline. Fair Turner's reply reached half way, and then a succession of scrimmages found the homesters on the aggressive. Their stay at the Warrington quarters was of brief duration, for clever footwork by Berry, Donohue, and Frank Turner assisted, by passing en the part of the two Bates and HPPSOM, resulted in Mossley falling back on their lines, a position they occupied almost throughout the match. A too close attention on the part of Gidlow towards John Bats penalized the homesters, but Pickford slightly relieved, and Dakin,Donollue, and Handles were conepicious in the saving department. The game was lacking in brilliancy and it was not until the first half was over that anything like proper form was shown by the visitors. The Bates initiated several pretty bits of work, whilst Evans and Heesom were only just stopped on the verge of scoring. Boscow was called upon to respond to a long kick, and had the better of the deal, whilst the wide pass from Fair Turner travelled to Jolley, and Nietd was compelled to kick dead. Lorrimer was prominent with a couple of kicks and passed the half way flag, and stimulated by this success the Mossley men pressed still, and eventu• ally reached the Warrington line. The danger was speedily cleared by John Bate, and from a long throw out Heesom got possession, and passing by the same player and Evans took the oval to the Mossley line once more. Fair Turner tried a shot at goal with no success, whilst a similar fate attended a pretty bit of passing by the Bates, Jolley and Evans, the first half-hour ending with no score. On resuming the homesters early showed signs ot distress, and the visitors allowed them no quar- ter. From the kick off the Warringtonians were on the attack, and James Bate was almost in the first minute. Evans was hampered by the close attention of Pickford, and though the latter got in a good kick to limner, the Warringtanian's safe return was instrumental in giving his side a better position. Berry led the forwards ably, whilst in one scrimmage Warrington screwed prettily, I but unfortunately lost possession and the visitors' line was eventually reached. It was left te Evans to change the venue, and a run three-parts the length of the field was followed by a pass to Heesom, which was not accepted. Jolley upset a movement of the homesters, whilst the Bates -11OVI asserted their superiority with no little reitult. The visitors continued to .force matters, and success came at last, for a determined rush by the I whole body of forwards pushed the Moseley set aside and I berry scored in a positioe which Fair Turner found too I difficult to convert from. Only a few minutes were now I required to complete the game, and during this time Mossley made a lest effort, and though a free kick looked dangerous James Bate met it in true style, and a grand run, feigning to pass during his progress' took play once more to the home quarter, where it rested when "no side" was called, Warrington winning by a try (three points). Teams :—Warrington : Boscow, back; Jones-, Evans and Beeson, three-quarters ; John Bate (captain) and James Bate, half backs; Fair Turner, F. Turner, Handles, Berry, Donohue. Iligham, Dakin, Lawless, and Thorniley, forwards. Mossley : Nield, back ; Pickford, F. Lorrimer and Wrigley, three-quarters ; J. Lorrimer and Gidlow, half backs ; Newby, Marian& Dawson. P. Walker, P. Hibbs, Woods, Corseadden, Coxand Marshall forwards. Referee, Mr. H. Eagles, Salford.

WARRINGTON A. V. MOSSLRY Betow FIRST TECADI.—. This match was played at Warrington, on Saturday. The homesters made a vigorous attack upon the vial- tore' lines, which culminated in Ashurst taking an op- ponent's pass and flopping over the line for Potts to place a capital goal. A splendid bit of combined work between Nevins, Potts, and Lawless ensued, the last named obtaining a fine try. Some grand passing be- tween Lally, Houghton, and Ashurst quite baill ol their opponents, Lawless scorine. easily behind the posts. Nevins kicking a goal. Bale' now scored for Moseley. No sooner had the game teen restarted than a splendid bit of play fairly roused th 3 spectators. Westall fol- lowed up a kick at top speed, succeeded in out-pacing his pursuers, and securing a grand try. Wilcox again scored for Warrington, and half time arrived soon after- wards. After the Borough Band had played their selec- tion of music the battle was resumed, Potts making a grand run right through his opponents, and passed to Bailey, who rushed over the line, and Weltall again galloped-in behind the posts, which Nevins converted. Teare and Lawless again scored, and the final was— Warrington, 5-4-2 ; Moseley, 0-1-0. , WARRINGTON Sr. MART'S V. REDFORD S. JOSRPIeS.—• Played at Warrington, on Saturday. Sanders started the ball for St. Mary's against a strong wind. The Bedford forwards, being rather too heavy for their op- ponents, took the ball to the home line,and Sale getting possession scored, the point not being improved. On restarting the visitors had the best of the game, and Shaughnessy dashed over and scored, the kick at goal failing. Half time arrived'vrith Bedford two tries, St. Mary's nil. On turning round Bedford held their own, ' but nothing being scored in the second- half,Bedford won by two tries to nil RUNCORN V. TIIIIRROOIC.—The preliminary canter in connection with the Runcorn Club took place on Satur- day, when they met Tuebrook on the Ir well-lane enclosure. The weather was beautifully fine, and if any- thing somewhat too warmespecially for the players. From a spectator's point of view it was agreeable and pleasant and to show their interest in the winter pastime a very large crowd surrounded the enclosure when the following teams took up their positions—Runcorn—S. Houghton, back; J. Crompton and Warder, three-quarter backs ; Rob neon and j. Faulkner, half backs ; Farmer, Davies, A. Tarler, Jordan, Keece, Shaw, Gayter, S. Taylor, Langley, forwards. Tuebrook—Jolley, back ; Lenton, E. Pearson, Meek, three-quarters; Green and Thompson, half backs ; Roberts, Holloway, C. Bayley, Holme,J. Pearson, H. Peirsin, It. Pearson, Steel and Green, fffr- wards. It was close upon 4.30 when Runcorn started the ball with the sun in their eyes. The visitors played up strongly at the start, and invading the home-quarters the ball was kicked dead across the Runcorn lines. This trifling success encouraged the Brookites, who scrim- maned vigorously and well, and for a time held a good position in midfield. At length Houghton broke away, an effort which W. Faulkner improved upon, and now a nice passing movement set in, which was eventually spoiled by an erratic pass. The home side, however, held their position, Warder and Houghton being con- spicuous in turn. At tenth Tuebrook regained mid- field, the harks displaying more smartness than had been expected. Each side enjoyed a free kick, but neither derived material advantage therefrom until Crompton dashed away and scored behind the posts with a

resulting goal from the same player's foot. On restart- ing Farmer headed a movement which let the Runcorn- tans within the visitors' 25. Crompton now dropped the tall over the bar, but unfottanately for the player the whistle had just previously been blown, and as a matter of course the point was not allowed. Warder also had a sideshot at goal from a free kick, which was within an ace of taking effect. A moment later J. Faulk- ner cameaway from a scrimmage, and ROW for a length of time the Brookites were heavily pressed. A moment later Crompton again scored, but failed to increase the value of the point. Desultory ptly ensued, after which Crompton from a pass by Warder, crossed the visitors' line for the third time, with a resulting goal. Nice pas- sing by the Runcornians was spoiled by the ball being thrown back. The scare at half time was—Runcorn, two goals, one try ; Tuebrook, one minor. The visitors having restarted, a good return by Faulkner enabled the home side to execute an aggrnsive movement which found the green jersies within the " Brook " 25, and although temporary relief was effected, Warder ran through a mass of players and scored, no goal accruing. The play still ruled in favour of Runcorn, and a mo- ment later Faulkner scored a further try, Crompton kicking his third goal. Subsequently, the visitors were rarely in the vicinity of the halfway line, and further tries were secured by Farmer (2), Crotnpton, Davies, Langley, and Warder, and as three of the points were improved upon by Crompton, Runcorn won by the large score of six goals and five tries to nothing. WIDNES V. RADCLIIME.—The opening of the football season was looked forward to with more than ordinary interest by Widnesians, and on Saturday afternoon about 2,000 spectators gathered on the Lowerhouse-lane ground to witness the contest between the local champions and Radcliffe Widnes started the game, with the sun shin- ing strongly in their faces. The visitors returned, and followed up, when the home men were awarded a free kick for off-side play. Alforde here made himself con- spicuous by a smart run, which slightly improved the home position. Brennan picked up, and after a splendid run passed to Clarke, who made a clear path along three-quarter the length of the field, and landed home safely amidst tremendous cheers. Play had only been restarted a few minutes when the ball was rushed up near the Radcliffe post, and Wynm3 placed the leather over. Half time Widnes 8 points; Radcliffe nil. In the last half Widnes had all the play and the final result WAS Widnes, four goals (one dropped), five tries, or 34 points; Radcliffe, nit. The teams were :—Widnes Full back, Brenann ; three-quarter backs, Barber, Alforde, Clarke; half backs, Drummond, and Wright; forwards, Whelan, Ridgway, Hogan, Fraser, Johnson, Derbyshire, Wynne, and Kenyon. laadcliffe : Back, Lowes ; three-quarter backs, Kent, Buseton, and Clegg ; half-backs, Barlow and Holt ; forwards, Nightingale, Bentley, Openshaw, Stewart, Rigby, Wardle, Jordan, Brown, and Olive.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday, in reply to a question respeoting our trade with Siam, Sir E. Grey said that under the treaty of 1855 British subjects were allowed equal participation in any privileges granted by the Siamese Government to the Goverament of any other nation.

Mr. A. MORLEY, questioned with reference to the tele- phone agreement, said the main object of it was the purchase of the trunk lines and the consequent with- drawal of the right of the Company to erect and work them.

Mr. GLADSTONE, in moving his resolution giving priority to Government business, said that after Supply and the Appropriation Bill had been disposed of they proposed to adjourn till November 2 for the autumn sitting. When the House met again it was their inten- tion to deal with the Parish Councils Bill and the Employers' Liability Bill, and he thought the time up to the Christmas holidays would be amply sufficient to dispose of these measures. Mr. BArFoun eonsidered an autumn sitting both unnecessary and inexpedient. A long discussion in which Mr. Chamberlain, Sir W. Har- court, and Mr. John Morley toak part, followed. Reference was made to the chances of the Eight Hours Bill for Miners and the Evicted Tenants Bill. The Chief Secretary said the latter bill could scarcely be discussed in a Saturday sitting. It was a matter that could only ,be dealt with by the . Government, and next year he hoped to place proposals before the House which would settle the question. An amendment moved by AIr.T.W. RUSSELL that the Saturday sittings should be limited to the sittings before the adjournment for the holidays was rejected by a majority of 40. Finally the closure was put, and Mr. Gladstone's resolution carried by a majority of 67. Tbe House then went into Committee of Supply.

In the House of Lords the Bishop of CHESTER moved that an address should be presented to the Queen asking her to withhold her assent to a part of the Cardiganshire intermediate education scheme, on the ground that it did great injustice, with regard to the scholarships, to the Voluntary schools affiliated to St. David's College, Lampter. The Earl of KIMIIERLEY said the scheme was not unjust. The only difference was this—Llandovery, one of the schools at which scholarships might ha held, was endowed ; Lampeter was not endowed. If Lampeter became an endowed school there was no reason why the matter stould not be reconsidered. The House divided, and the motion was carried by 33 votes against 23. The Bishop of CHESTER moved a similar address against a portion of the Merionethshire scheme, which, he said, introduced the undenominational system of teaching into the boarding-house. The Earl of Kimberley and the Lord Chancellor considered the scheme a fair com- promise. Lord SALISBURY spoke strongly against it. The motion was carried on a division by 39 votes to 23. Lord HOSEISERY, replying to a question, said Lord Dufferin had returned to Paris with the most ample in- structions to protect British rights in Siam.

TUESDAY. In the House of Lords on Tuesday, Eirl SPENCER moved the second reading of the Home Rule Bill. Coercion, he said, would never cure discontent, and he was afraid the measures that had been passed for the benefit of Ireland had been introduced without due regard to the sentiments of the Irish people. Among that people there was a passion for self-government, and after his experience in administering law in Ireland on the old methods he had faith in the new colicy. The financial arrangements in the Home Rule Bill were

EARLESTO WN HOMING SOCIETY.

The open raCe from Swindon was flown -on the 19th August, for the West Lancashire Cup. gm birds belonging to 91 fanciers competed. Mr. Thomas Howarth liberated at 1017, wind S.W. by W.N. T. Hopkin of Swindon kindly examined seals and signed usual certificates. Mr. Barron won the cup, value £5 55., presented by the president Mr. H. J. Longton. . The first six divide the entry fees in money prizes, according to the rules, and the next eleven take diplomas. Result : , yards . THE SUICIDE OF AN EARLESTOWN

li&N.

LETTERS OF THE SUICIDE. AN OPEN VERDICT.

Mr. J. F. Price, coroner, of the Salford District, held an inquest at the Black-a-Moors Head Hotel, Rivington, on Saturday, upon the body of Thomas Knowles, 45, tailor and woollen draper, of Queen- st., and Earle-at., Earlestown, who committed sui- cide by drowning in " Little" Lodge, belonging to the Liverpool Corporation on the previous Thurs- day.—Thomas Molyneux, grocer and provision dealer, Market-st.. Earlestown, stated the deceased was his brother-in-law, and was 45 years of age and had always enjoyed good health. He left home on the Friday owing co money troubles. Witness had a conversation with deceased about a month ago, when the latter told him abut his financial difficulties, and he advised him what to do, but he did not follow his advice on any one point. Had dersia styl done so he believed things would have gone on all right. He last saw deceased on the Tuesday,at his shop door in Earles- town, when he bade him" Good morning" and passed on. On the Friday deceased complained to his wife of being upset and left the house without telling her where he was going. His wife told him that she had advised him to go for a walk, and nothing was heard of him un- til they learned of his death. They traced him to Liver- pool, on the Sunday,and was told he had slept at Waver- tree on the Friday night. He heard that he had been to the docks, and witness thought he had gone to America. When he had the conversation with him deceased said he would either drown or make away with himself. Deceased had had some drink but ap- peared hopeful when he left him.—By the Foreman : Deceased bad not been an intemperate man, but had given way to drinking latterly. Deceased was solvent. He was certain he had no troubles outside his business. —Abraham Thornley, clerk, Great House, Riviaigton, stated that he had known deceased about six years. On the Saturday previous deceased paid him an unexpected visit. Be did not appear as lively as usual. He spent his time going walks, and on Monday visited Horwich and Chorley for the purpose of collecting some debts,and on returning said he had got promises but no money. When he came he said he had made it up with his wife to go away for a day or two for a holiday, and decided r,o come to see witness, he brought no luggage. He com- plained about trade being bad through the colliers being out on strike, but said nothing more about money matters. On Tuesday morning deceased bade him " Cood morning" and witness thought he was leaving but on returning home at night he found him in the house. The following day deceased said he felt better in health and spirits than he had done for a long time. He conversed with him and they read the newspapers from 8-30p.m. to 11-15 pan.on the Wednesday. At 7.25 on Thursday morning witness's wife came upstairs exclaiming " Knowles' " Knowles" and informed him that deceased had left some letters on the table. Witness went down stairs and found the two letters produced. The writing was in pencil and written on printed bill head forms. One of these read, - Dear Bro. Thornley, I have gone to the water round the Black Boy, and you will find my body just past the third tree. Remember me to my wife and family and tell (here the sentence was left unfinished). I can't wait any longer and may the Lord have mercy on my soul. Wire them at once' your brother, Thomas Knowles. The other docucnentread "Dear Brother Thornley,—Accept my: thanks for your kindness. lam weary of waiting, hoping you will let my family know. Yours &G., Thomas Knowles. 1 have put the key under the door. On the other side of this note was written" Good nye and God bless my family and all friends." Witness left the house and went to the Little Lodge, where they found the body_pf deceased Deceased was without coat, hat and collar. He was in no way related to deceased.—Tbe Coroner summed up and remarked upon the want of evidence to show that deceased was in an unsound state of minel.—The fore- man was of opinion that there was not sufficient evid- ence to show that deceased was of unsound mind.— The other Jurors agreed and an "Open Verdict" was re- turned.

GOOD FOR TRADERS.—If there is anything which men in the Commercial World are supposed to bc alive to, it is their own interests. Forabout eight years now they have had Mr. Henry Sell, or Fleet-street, working hard to give them a Directory of Telegraphic Addresses,which should be as complete as possible as a result of personal labour, but which could only be quite complete if he could transcribe the Post Office Register of those ad- dresses. For these eight years Mr. Sell has striven, try- ing four Postmasters-General in succession, and at last Eucceeding with Mr. Arnold Morley. The next edition will contain more than double the number of addresses ever previously given, will be authoritative as it comes from the Post Office direct, and will certainly be about the most valuable publication ever issued for business men. Surely !hay will see that Mr. Sell has a little reward for what he has done by buying his book, and making a permanent addition to their books of re- ference.

BMIGRITION.—Intending emigrants to New York, Bosten Philadelphia, San Francisco, Canalax, China, Japan, Ave tralla, or New Zealand, should take ti ,kets (lowest rates) at the " Examiner " Office, by Guion, American, Australian Oriental, and other llnes.—John Walker, agent,"Examiner Orace, liewsty-st, Warrlngtaa.—Acirt•

DEATH OF COLONEL IRELAND- BLACKBURNE.

Colonel J. Ireland-Blackburne, of Hale Hall, died in London on Tuesday morning. He had been ill for some time. He was about seventy-six years of age, and had suffered from blindness for some years. He was a tice of the peace for Lancashire, and was a kind anti considerate landlord and very popular. The deceased was the representative of a family distinguished in both ancestral lines in matters military, diplomatic, social, and scientific. Be was born on the 28th May, 1817,. so that he had outspanned by six years the alloted three score years and ten. He spent his early days in the an- cestral home of the Blackburne's, Orford Hall, Warring- ton, where, in his early manhood, InS formed a close intimacy with his neighbour, Mr. John Wilson Patten, of Bank Hall, afterwards Lord Winmarleigh, also lately deceased. This intimacy continued through all the different spheres of their lives, and when both resided in Warrington, they went hand in hand in matters in fur-1 tlierance of the social. interests of the neighbourhood. Colonel Blackburne was early conversant with matters political, for his grandfather did not retire from political life till 1830, and in 1835 his father Mr. John Ireland Blackburne successfully contested Warrington. While his father was attending to the political interests of War- rington from 1837 to 1848,be having been again returned in the former year, the son was undergoing his scholastic and military training. A hitherto unpublished incident, of the great O'Connell may fittingly find expression at this time. Meeting Mr. John Ireland Blackburne, who of course, as an out-and-out Tory, was diametrically opposed to him politically, in the lobby of the House of Commons, Dan O'Connell, with that ready wit:of which he was so great a master, Said "Mr. Ireland Blackburne, you are so fond of the name of Ireland that I know you will frank this letter to Ireland for me." The request Was instanter complied with. The political life of the son did not begin until the death of Mr. Turner, when he succeeded him in the representation of South West Lancashire as a colleague to Lord (then Mr. Cross). He continued in this capacity until the redistribution of seats in 1885, after which he did not seek to re-enter Parliament, but subsequently passed a retired life in London. He was Lord of the Manors of Warrington and Hale, and colonel of the 4th Lancashire Militia. He was a member of Parliament for South-West Lancashire until the Redistribution Bill. He is succeeded by the his son, Lieutenant-Colonel Robert Ireland-Blackburn. . . . .

By his first wife Mary, daughter of the late Sir Henry Bald Houghton, Bart., of Houghton Towers, Lancashire, whom he married in 1846, there survives Lieutenant Colonel Ireland-Blackburne, Mrs. Heywood Bright, of West Derby ; Mrs. R. C. Antrobus, and Lady Edwardes- Moss. There were no children of his marriage in 1857 to Emma Jernima, daughter of Mr. James Ravenscroft, and widow of the fifteenth Viscount Hereford. This lady predeceased him in 1870. The deceased Colonel was an ardent Conservative and Churchman. One of his latest benefactions was the free grant of the site for the erection of new schools in Manchester-road, Warrington. For many years be was president of the Warrington Infirmary and a trustee of the Warrington Blue Coat School. As a private gentleman and landowner he was held in high esteem, and his demise, though not unexpected, will be sincerely regretted. Colonel Blackburne's military career began in 1838 in the sth Dragoon Guards, with whom he rerved for four- teen years, during the latter part of which period he was a captain of the regiment. 1852 he was placed at the command of the 2ad Lancashire Militia, and in the troublous times immediately preceding and during the Crimean War he threw himself energetically into the for- mation of the 4th Royal Lancashire Militia, in the raising of which he was mainly instrumental. It was while this regiment was being raised that he came most in touch with the people of Warrington, his military figure being then one of the most conspicuous in the streets of Warrington. Many lively recollections of this period, in which he chiefly figured, are stored up in the minds of Warringtonians. As a mark of recognition of his ser- vices in this cause and appreciation of his intrittsic worth as an officer, he was appointed Lieutenant-Colonel of his regiment. his connection with it was unbroken until he finally retired from the service with the rank of Colonel.

• WARRINGTON LADS ON THE CLIO."

A deputation from the Warrington School Attendance Committee visited the ship "Clio" on the 6th July. There were, they report, 33 boys from Warrington on the ship, and many of them were questioned, and with- out exception they appeared well contented with the life on board the ship. Several boys are about to leave the ship, their time having expired, and the members were pleased to find that with one exception the lads were intending 0 join the Merchant service. The deputation was conducted through the ship by the courteous com- mander, Captain Moger, and every department was in- spected. The boys in the school and at work in the various departments, shoemaking,tailoring, baking &c. appeared to be industrious and well conducted. No- thing csuld be better than the result of the recent examination of the lads. The total percentage of passes of the 266 boys examined was 99.2. With regard to the ship, the members again desire to express the entire satisfaction which the inspection afforded them. A cheque for the year's donation was handed to the captain, and the members again contributed £1 towards the boys' fund. A deputation also visited the Boys' Refuge, Liverpool, on the 11th July. Superintendent Pridgen kindly con- ducted the deputation through the Institution. The Warrington boys, 21 in number, were inspected and questioned by the members. They seemed in good health. The dormitories have been very much im- proved. proper regard having been paid to the ventila- tion of the rooms and the provision of means of escape in case of file. Under the able management of Superin- tendent Pridgen rnany improvements have been effected, not only in the arrangements of the school, but in the treatment of the boys, and the deputation were pleased to learn that a scheme which has already received the sanction of the Government Department is on foot of providing a Home in North Wales as a health and holi- day resort to which the boys will be sent from time to time. The Government Inspector made a most favour- able report of the school, and stated "that the boys have creditably passed in all the usual subjects." An interesting discussion took place on the above report at the meeting of the Town Counoil, reported in another column.

Cardinal Vaughan, who succeeded Cardinal Manning as Archbishop of Westminster, has seven brothers in the priesthood, and three sisters who are nuns. The Rev. Benjamin Waugh is thus described in the " Phrenological Magazine :—Flis frontal angle shows a marked degree of intellectual power and scope of mind. His head is high from the opening of the car to the coronal region, which gives him high aspirations, philanthropic desires, and inclination to work for moral purposes. He has an organisation which indicates spirit and hope.

THE WARRINGTON ,ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL

CLUB.

Efir,—With reference to the above club, Ihe to In- form lovers of the Asscciation game in Warrington and district that the Warrington A.F.C. have dissolved in favour of the Cricket A.F.C., and the members of the former have decieled to realise the" stock in trade,"and together with a fair balance which the club had to their credit, hand the sum over to the local Infirmary. We do not wish to add to this resolution, but we leave it to the sportsmen of Warrington for their commendation or otherwise, as they think we deserve. All we wish for is that the game should prosper whether on the cricket field or elsewhere, in Warrington, and as this step will tend to centralise the strength, we think it is a step in the right direction. Hoping the Association game may have its fair share of support. Yours, Etc., JAMES MATFIER. late secretary Warrington A.F.C.

DISTRESS IN WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me through the medium of your valuable paper to express my regret that the coal strike is being so long protracted. It is no doubt caus- ing in our midst an amount of distress for which the Warrington working-man is not himself resnonsible. It is a duty we all owe to do our little best to mitigate as far as we are able the distress which seems to be growing. Any case of real distress that may exist in St. John's Ward—(t hat being the part of the town with which lam best acquainted) I shall be pleased to do what I can to afford relief of a practical character. Any &servinn, case must be-made known to me at my office Haydock- st., Warrington, between three and four o'clock on Tues- day, Thursday, and Friday, when it shall receive my attention. Thanking you for the insertion,—l remain yours, Sze., Poplars, Great Sankey, Sep. 7,1893.

MINISTERS AND CHOIRS.

===== 0002601_18930909_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
W. FURNESS.

Sir,—l had just come fresh from a small article in my " Musical Times " for this month, on the subject of musical feeling without knowledge, wherein the writer marvels that the curious notion still exists that the musical world is divided into those who feol, but do not know, and those who know but do not feel ; while the fact that there is a third class, namely, those, who, able to feel, have also learnt to knowond who knowing.have yet not ceased to feel, is altogether ignored. With the remark that ignorance is not always a necessary condi- tion of bliss,and that all attempts to prove the superiority of feeling to knowledge, or of knowledge to feeling, are not only useless, but mischievous, since, " nothing so effectually conceals a whole truth as the exhibition un- der limelight of half of it," the article closes. It was with feelings in full sympathy with these stimulants that I attended the reception meeting given to the new Wesleyan ministers at the Bold-st.. School- room on Monday evening last. Now I wish to keep in mind that my present dealings are with a minister of religion, hut he has thrown down the gauntlet in such truly pugnacious fashion ,and has declared wit Is iconoclas- tic blare, that while he was delighted with music at one chapel.if he finds that there is anything of a scientifir(that word seems to be his bete noire) nature it the other chapel, 11,011 see to it, Sze.,ke. I think he will pardon me if, fresh from his speech he finds that I have caught some of his spirit:

*To begin with I should like to say that there is no narrow or personal object in my letter, as rezards the musical service of the two places of worship. For good reasons tam interested equally in both. My only reason for writing is to ventilate a antisct that seems to me to require ventilation.. It is a very serious and important matter, this question of music in dissenting places vf worship, and I much deplore the ill-timed speech, when other denominations are worthily endeavouring to be abreast of the times, when every schoonlbny is being equipped to take on honourable (I bad almost said " scientific ") part in the divine art. It is all very well to glow about Methodism at its rise being carried on a wave of song from John o'Grost's to Land's End ; everybody understands and acknowh‘ges the glorious part music had in that great revival of re- ligion, and I for one am proud of it ; but when, on that account we are told that we must stick to the good old tunes, and call the organist a villain for introducing a new tune, I submit that a word of protest is called for,

, I was reading in the "Methodist Times " lately a leader on preaching, and the special point taken up was that if ministers are to preach with tongues of fire, they must not be content to deliver twenty year old sermons, but to keep continually abreast of current thought—and it seems to me that this idea exactly applies to the musi- cal portion of the service also. 80, I ask (and I should be glad of an answer), the young man of the present day to be pinned down to the music of his forefathers, because, forsooth, the music of a century and a half ago performed its mission so well then ? It would pain me much to he misunderstood in this matter, and I will say at once that no one has a greater delight in some of the good old tunes, which are, and I hope for its own sake always will be, inseparable from Methodism— hut, as a wise man has said, " while we reverence the old we must bring a warm heart to the new," and it is only a tendency to narrow mindedness, a bane at all times, which lam now combating. _

My present contention is this ; that so long as ministers persist in assuming a dictatorial attitude towards choirs and organists, treating them with such scant respect—. so long as the necessary feeling of true sympathy and charity is swallowed up in a spirit 01 arrogance—so long will a blight hang over the true mission of music in our churches. Flow different that feeling and spirit dis- played last Sunday evening but one, by the revered pastor of he Congregationalist Church in Bewsey-st., at a musical service there. All hrmour to him for the words of wisdom he used on that occasion !

I will commend to our ministers the following quota- tion from Curwen's " Studirs in Worship Music " in the August number of the " Wesleyan Methodist Church Record," and there recommended as worth ponderino. : " Put no honour upon the office of choirsinger, let the choir feel no special spiritual responsibility, and the result is relaxing. G- eat them as coil( azues and assis- tants of the minister, lot prayer he offered for them from time to time, lose no opportunity of dwelling on the spiritual motive which should underlie their musical work, and the choir must inevitably rise to a better per- formance of its duties."—Yours truly,

SPECTATOR.

WEEKLY ILEPOET OF THE HEALTII OF

WARRINGTON.

During the week, which was the 35th of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered :—Births : Warrington, 45 ; Latchford, 5; total, 50. Deaths : Warrington, 31; Latchford, 1, total 32. The excess of births over deaths was 18. The death-rate from all causes was equal t 0302 per 1.000, against 278 the average of the pre.vious lour weeks, and against 22.1 the corresponding week sf last year. The death-rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 179 per 1.000, against 12.2. the average rate of the tour previous weeks,and against 71 the corresponding week of 1892. The ages of mortality were ;—Warrington :0 to 1, 11. 1; 1 to 5,14 ; 5 to 15, 2; 15 to 25, 0 ; 25 to CO, 3 ; 60 up 1 ;total, 30-1 32. The deaths included-2 from from measles ; 3 :from scarlet fever : 11 from diarrhcea ; 1 from entric fever; 2 from whoop- ing cough ; 2 from tubercular diAease; 1 from cancer ; 3 from brain disease; 1 from lung disease ; 1 from liver disease. The infectious disease have been reported during the week :- Bcarlet fever, 17 ; enteric I. Meteorolog3.—The mean temperature was 57.4 Fah. and therefore 4i. deg. lower than the preceding week. The maxi- mum temperature 69 F., was recorded on the 30th inst., and the minimum temperature 40 F., on the 28th inst. The rainfall measured 0.22 in. The general direction was N.VV., the reading of the anemometer denoting 835 miles.—Jons; H. GonsIALL, Medical Oilicer of Health.

ATHLETICS.

NUTP.B ON U.2-killES A:ND

CRICART.

There is yet another defeat to chronicle against War- rington first eleven, for they have been defeated for the second time this season by Birkenhead Park by 163 to 86 The team must be glad that the cricket season is almost over as it has been a very disastrous one for them. They made a better show against Birkenhead Park on Saturday,however,than at the first meeting which was a sorry exhibition indeed. Their highest scorer was R.Smith with only 16,closely followed by Mortimer 15,and Griffin 14. George Clarke did not make a "duck" this time as he notched a dozen. C. Holden on the other side beat War- rington himself as he made 91 before he was sent back. A mysterious " Hampson" was the most effective bowler for Warrington his four wickets only costing 24 rnns. 'The remaining six wickets were taken by J. J. Crosfleld for 77 runs.

Warrington 2nd alsa finished on the wrong side on Saturday. They too have done very poorly, and last Saturday's result read Birkenhead Park 94 for eight wickets, Warrington 84 all out. The fielding and the bowling was very good, or the batsmen were out of form for only three Warrington men got into double fi,,ures. Unsworth with 18 being top scorer. It is very inexplic- able how the Warrington teams, first and second, have such bad luck, as there are undoubtedly some very good crickcters amongst them. However, the end of the season has almost arrived, and let us hope for better things next time.

Sankey Wire Mills beat Cairo-street Unity on Satur- day, but low scoring was the rule, the only score of any consequence being Lightfoot for the Unity, who made 15. The result was 38 to 27 for the Wire Mills. Hobson for the Wire Mills had the excellent bowling average of eight wickets for 12 runs. •

FOOTBALL.

The cricket season has now come to an end, and the eight months football season has commenced. that football is far more popular among the masses than cricket was well shown last Saturday, when " gates "of League teams in most places amounted to over tea thousand. Where would they get such a splendid " gate " as this in a cricket match ? The cause of this is not very difficult to find. Cricket, although an ex- ceedingly lice game, is rather slow, and unless a " slogger " is at it, it is not very apt to raise the enthu- siasm of the crowd. On the other hand football abounds in exciting play. At almost every minute of the game there is something exciting going on, and the spectator is continually on the tenter hooks of expectancy, to the very last moment of the match.

Coming to the doings of the Warrington football team, it was the will of the fates that the first team should fight their first battle upon "foreign soil." Mossley as a rule have not been a very easy team to beat under their own vine and fig tree, so that a victory over them by a try to nil is nct a bad start. This result,however, is not as satisfactory as the play. During fully fifty minutes of the sixty played, the Warringtonians were pressing the Mossleyites upon their lines, and Warring- ton had exceedingly hard lines in missing scoring many times. The player who had the distinction to draw first blood for Warrington this season was Berry.

A try to nil this season is a greater victory than it was last, as now a try counts three points against two last season. The individual play on Saturday was very good, and Boscow was as safe as ever. Jolley in his unusual place—at least of late years—of three-quarter back, came off very well. " Captain" Bate and "Smack" Bate were also as agile behind the scrimmage as ever, while the forwards pushed the Mossley forwards pretty much as they pleased during the last half.

How will Warrington get along in the Lancashire Rugby League this season? They should make a good bid for the championship. They have plenty of players to fall back upon should any of the older ones not feel equal to the occasion,as the "A" team match on Saturday proved when they won by five goals, four tries to a try. Of course it is a very stiff climb up the league ladder with such clubs as Swinton, Salford, Wigan, Tyldesley, Ste., to meet, but the team have beaten everyone with the exception of Salford,and they can Ao it again. Warrington "A" were determined there should he no mistake as to the result of their match with Mossley Brow Ist,on Saturday. They actually won by five goals, four tries to one try, or 37 points to three. Needless to say that after the first ten minutes, Mossley were com- pletely at the mercy of their opponents. Lawless has early developed scoring powers, as three tries were ob- tained by him. Westall, however, was the hero of the match. His running powers were well shown, and I should think that he is worthy to be placed in the first team, to whom his speed would be a great acquisition. Potts and Nevins were also largely instrumental in win- ning the game. Lally was as quick as ever behind the scrimmage, and had a good helper in Hughes.

CYCLING.

On Saturday last,ls Members of the Warrington Cycl- ing Club ran out to Lostock Oralam and from thence via Cranage, home a splendid afternoon being spent. J. Waddington on that day tried to lower the 100 miles Cheshire roads records. Owing to not being well paced at the start ho failed just to beat the record by eight minutes his tinie being six hours five minutes, a really grand performance when we consider the drawbacks. On Tuesday, E. rickavance, jun., along with Messrs. Adams and W. Deakin of the Anfield 8.C., met Mr. Lawrence Fletcher (who is trying to lower the Land's End to John O'Groats record) at Warrington and paced him through Wigan, Preston and Lancaster to Kendal, a good lift no doubt over the rough roads. On Tuesday evening, a score of members turned out and had a good run to Rainhill.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1893. CIVILIZATION'S INFERNO. 'WA IiRINGTON SLAUGHTER BANK QUAY SOAP WORKS. HOUSES.

BOW LING MAIN. The last bowling main of the season in connec- tion with the Bank Quay Soap Works, Warrington. was held at the Cemetery Inn bowling green, on Satifrday afternoon. The competition was for a splendid lot of prizes presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfield. Some of the tics proved to be very excit- ing, the final between J. Barton and J. Bandies, being , especially so, and Barton was deservedly cheered when he ran out victor. After the competition was over, the prizes were presented on the green, by Dr. Markel', in the unavoidable absence of Mr. A. H. Crosfield.—Mr. Willett, in introducing the doctor explained that Mr. Crosfield had senc him a letter expressing his great regret at not being able to be present, as he was engaged in a medal competition in golf. This being the case he (Mr. Willett) was glad that they had got Dr. Markel,' to take his place.—Dr. Markel' who was received with applause, said that it gave him great pleasure to be present that afternoon and to present tho prizes to the winners. Ile was rather in an awkward position as he was expected to speak upon a game he had never played. He, how- ever, took a great interest in it. He might say that in coming on the green .he heard someone say that a foreigner was to give the prizes that afternoon. Well he had the had taste to have chosen Germany for his birth- , place--(laughter)—but still he could prove that he was a better Englishman than the man who had called him a foreigner. That man was born in England because he could not help it, but he (Dr. Markell) was an English • man from choice. (Laughter.) Continuing the doctor expressed his pleasure at meeting the workmen outside the works. He could assure them that next to his interest in the firm, which was paramount with him, he took an interest in those who worked for the firm (Applause.) The doctor then presented the prizes which were as follows :—First prize J. Barton, Case of knives and forks ; second prize J. Randal's, clock ; third prize G. W. Wilcock, clock ; there were also other prizes. After the presentation of prizes, Mr. Willett in pro- posing a vote of thanks to the doctor for his presence at the main, said that he thought that this main had been one of the best and most crderly that they had played during the whole season and no one had lost his temper. tie was very sorry that Mr. Crosfleld could not be with them as he took a deep interest in the doings of the workmen. He thought that he was echoing the senti- ments of everyone present in saying that they were truly thankful for Mr. Crosfield's kindness and generosity towards them. Mr. Crosfield had come among them and by this time had learnt all about their ways. He hoped that during the coming winter the social evenings would prove a success. He expected that they would com- mence next month. He might say that the idea of these social evenings was first suggested on thegreen on which they stood, and they had by the generosity of Mr. Cros- field, a piano presented to them. Let them make the evenings a success during the coming winter. In con- clusion he thanked Dr. Markell for his kindness that afternoon in being present.—The motion was seconded and carried with three cheers for the docter.—Dr. Markel' thanked the men for their vote, and expressed a desire to take a few lessons in bowling, as he had already had some in cricket. They might depend upon it that if he was beaten at first, they might look out after a few games. (Laughter.) The doctor then related some amusing experiences in cricket. Subsequently a jovial meeting was held at the Committee room in the Cemetery Inn at which the programme consisted of songs, etc.

(BY MR. H. WHITHAm.)

Well now, what is the cause of all this desolation? You in England are pretty well used to it. I may say the same of the whole of Europe I think, I suppose you are having a big strike of the miners jusr now, you have only just settled the cotton strike. I saw in a recent letter of " Cyrnro's" to the" Examiner" on the coal trouble a noteworthy ' line in which he said that the supply exceeded the demand. Ile may be right in saying this during July, but I am afraid he would have said the same had it been December. Why does the supply exceed the demand? I have looked over the latest list of new coal pits opened in England, and I was quite surprised ; their number is phenominal. As the." York " says,." There must be money in it." . .

What a lot of 'fools we are surely. The boss at Home- stead says "improved machinery gluts the market." The miner says "opening more pits gluts the coal market." The cloth and cotten manufacturer says "the workhouses are all full, shut down a month." The bankers, and brokers, the thieves of Wall-street, and the good old lady of Threadneedle-street say "there is too much silver, stop coining it" in India, and stop mining it in Colorado, and for answer to all this we open a new silver mine in Australia that turns out 200,000 ozs. a month. We go on putting up improved machinery in our mills, and factories. The best of it is "we are all quits sure that it must be done, and do you know ? we shall have to shut up." The workingman is a turbulent, dissatisfied fellow, always striking, and agitating for less hours. We want to run our machinery all the hours God sends, don't talk to u 3 about limiting the output. If we grant you less hours, you will have to do as Mather and Platts very sensible workmen have done, squeeze ten hours work into nine." Looking through the last issue of the Labour Gazette, London, I find that in Belgium a large league is being formed to petition the employers to close their shops at noon on Sundays and public holidays. This is certainly going too far, just to think that the Continental workmen want half a day's rest on God's holy day! Why these men deserve to be— Well the fact is, they are selfish, and not considerate enough of their employer's happiness. In all seriousness, we must ask the cause of these troubles, and how shall we get rid of them? I remember Max Muller settling the question as to what we should do with the old folks. He suggests they be taken into the woods, and left for the wild beasts to settle matters with. The only objection I see to this is the growing scarcity of wild beasts. Young folks grow old so fast, that the four-footed beast has to give place to his slightly more upright supplanters and so, of course, only the fittest survives. But what shall we do with the young men and women ? That there E.t.a plenty around, no one denies. It is of no avail to answer, that there is plenty of food, plenty of clothing, and enough of shelter for all. This is no answer, because a political economy that had its birth with Adam Smith and James Watt has decreed that those who toil not, nor yet spin shall be the right- ful owners of all earned and unearned increment. It was suggested some years ago that some sort of a round- about Balthusian system might be taught the people, so that the supply of labour should be limited. But that was objectionable to capital, besides, was it not agai fly the law, and the prophets ? I am pleased to seethe answer is fast taking shape in the minds of men. For years I, and others have done our hest to Stamp out the wild, selfish and unreasoning competition that fills the minds of men. I know the arguments our opponents use. They are stale as hard tack, and there is not a manufacturer or tradesman in any line of business but will allow these arguments to be utterly worthless. Otto Stechhan of Indianapolis, Ind., the newly elected president of the National Furniture Manufacturers' Association, at a recent convention held in St. Louis, Mo., gave expression to opinions which are of great interest to employer, and employee alike. He claims to have gained his knowledge through personal investigation while holding a position on the State Board for industrial schools. Mr. Stechhan said : " Competi- tion at one time was considered the life of trade, but this maxim has long ago become a fallacy and proves, if anything, to be a veritable curse instead of a blessing. Every 'Mots., 10dols.. yes, 15101 g., chamber suit or 3 50do1s. lounge or 1.25d015. bedstead which is placed on the mallet to-day is a curse to the community, not referring to its poor intrinsic value. It 'means that labour has been degraded and pulled into the mire; that it has been brought on a level with convict labour ; that we are praducing a new race of slaves. It means that thousands of children are taken away from school at a time when they should be acquiring useful knowledge; that they are wearing out their young lives in the shops and factories, in the dust, grime and smoke. It means an im povished race ; it means slavery in its worse form. Mr. Stechhan went on to say further that he csuld vouch for the following, and I paiiicularly call atten- tion to the facts stated :—" I know of a concern which has the enviable reputation of manufacturing extremely cheap chamber suits which refused to give employment to skilled labourers who applied for work, because, as the firm members expressed themselves, the applican.s expectei to receive one dollar per day, which, consider- ing that they had a family to support, would be as cheap as they could possibly afford to work,because the , concern was only paying 80 cents per day for skilled labour; that even such hands as worked by the piece rarely earned one dollar per day. Another instance parallel with those quoted came to my ears which as- tonished me all the more because it was perpetrated by an establishment considered incapable of such an action. When we consider, however, that the margins have with this concern also become so small that it instructs its salesmen to sell only a certain amount of one parti- cular kind of its manufacture—beyond which it could afford to pay a commission—then we can readily see why such discreditable actions are possible. A la- bourer who had been employed by this concern for many years, who wore himself out in its employ at the munificent salary of I. dollar 25 cents per day, recently had his wages reduced. because, as Was told him, needed improvements were necessary, and the firm could in consequence not pay such high wages. The real cauFe, however, was that the man was getting old and possibly was no fonger able to do the same amount of work as in hi i younger days. In view of the fact that the proprietors of this concern are considered inde- pendently wealthy,and occupy a front seat hi church, it is a matter of easy solution why we hear of discontent in labour circles. . . .

So far as I can gather, this sort of thing ia going on in every trade. It is practfeed,however.more particularly on the poor ignorant people corning front the European continent. Again, it in to be noted that there are amongst these ignorant workers coming here a pretty large per centage who have imbibed the worst features, or should I say, teachings of the continental socialists. These teachers themselves are not wanting. They do not work, but manage to live on the earnings of others, seeking every opportunity for spreading their pernicious doctrines. It has been noticed' that the shooting and dynamite work at the Homestead riots was all perpet- rated by men who knew not a word of Engliisb,. arid had not the least conception of the power of the ballot box. I hold that the road to emancipation lays right through the ballot box, nor have they other knowledge but what lies in brute force and corrupt polities. The practical outcome of this is, employers are beginning to see that in inviting cheap labour frouvEusope, they have•invited a class of men that may at any time fall upon, and rend them, therefore the.edict has gone forth from more than one large firm that no more Hungarians are to be employed. Society, like the employers,.is beginning to tremble, and ask itself the pertinent question, what shall it do to be saved? No one will accuse Ward McAllister of de- voting too much of his time to people's question. As leader of the "Four Hundred '" Nis time must be pissed in upper sweatdoin, yet he has found time to puishsh a letter in which he says " Much, of our trouble lies in our always discounting the future.. If a piece of nroperty is worth to-day £lOO,OOO we expect in a few years it will , double or treble, and at once estimate ourselves at this ratio. There is no country in. the world in which the cost of living is so great as in, ours. Our daily expen- diture for simple necessities is absurdly extravagant. Since the millionaire era the vost of living with las has been quadrupled. The millionaires are responsible, to a great extent, for this burdenlaid upon people possessing moderate incomes. They have initiated extravagant expenditure in everything. Up go their costly palaces in town and country, stablvs that must needs h.hl 25 to 30 horses, a retinue of servants required by a king, a head cook whose wages are equivalent to Use salary of the Governor of one of oat states, the maintrnanee of a yacht for six months at an expense that would have paid Gen. Washington' salary as President af the United States. "All this is increasing day by da.v, until we are brought to a standstill by the daily reports of crash after crash of the falling fortunes of our comntrysnen. It then, not so much stopping the scare," as our great politician suggests, as finding a remedy ad turning over a new leaf; going stow, instead of keeping up this break-neck p.m: initiated by imaginary fortunes of ml lions on paper. There are three distinct features I have tried to bring out in this letter. The direful poverty of the poor. Th” evils of the competitive system, snit the nienac,. to the whole social fabric existing in an unreasonably rich plutocracy. Here, as in England, the people as a body do not appreciate at anything like its value the power of the ballot box. Politics are so corrupt that it mping as if the only method of reform lies in the direction of !e- -volution. 'lse Amman stable needs the treinenclous rind overwhelming stream of popular indignation and revolt to clean jC, Sellis'iness that compels poor men to pay 103. per week for three small, stuffy, unsanitary rooms in the city, or, as in my case. 14s. per week out of a miser- able wage of 65. per day is not to be borne much longer.

Lord Shannon, who has been called the "Cowboy Lord," is a unique typl of Irishman. His wild life in America gave him a tare for rough men and hard la bc,ur, and he spends much of his time in his beAutifill country seat in county Cork wi,ll his own in the lidds. Ile particularly fond 8f wod-cliopping, an.] Ike results of his labours in this reaps are ahvays distributed as fm.l to his cottager, who, it can easily to imagined, are devoted to their landlord.

CLAIMS OF MORALITY.

(By "CLEo.") Since the publication of last week's article, I have seen the report of the Chief Inspector of Nuisances in the medical officer's report for the year ending 31st December, 1892. Referring to the slauchter houses he says "As I have remarked in my previous reports, the inspection of slaughter houses, which are spread over the borough, is a matter of great difficulty, and the only way of simplifying the labour of inspection would be accomplished by the erection of a public slaughter house and abattoir." Here is ample official and, as far as I am concerned, independent corroboration of the fact that such an institution is a dire necessity. The requirements and claims of morality are little less urgent than the demands of health for the abolition of the present slaughter houses. Ido not wish to hurt sensitive nerves by too minute discrip- tion, nor do I wish to bedaub this picture with the gruesome details that go on within the walls of these houses, but the necessity of having the full light of public opinion directed towards them if for no other purpose than to reveal the moral depravity to which many of our children are being exposed, is so clamorous that I must make this a miniature chapter of horrors. How many in War- rington, may I ask, are cognizant of the tact that boys and children have an almost free access to some of the slaughter houses when slaughtering is going on ? If there be any such, then I tell you to your faces that you have been lacking in your duty not to have raised your voices in condemnation of such a practice. Think of thern,from the tiniest dot of childhood till well on in their teens, being debasingly fami- larised with the sufferings and the bloodshedding of the slaughter-houses, and then take your share in any movement to remove this influence from their midst. I speak with no Equearnieh feelings at the sight of blood, for I have had more scenes of horror in my history than perhaps any man in Warrington. I stood beside and gazed with awe- struck horror on the mutilated features of a mur- dered wife and mother and her murdered little ones in that demon's house at Rainhill, I ransacked that charnel house from top to bottom, including the awful ki:chen ; 1 have seen the distorted, torn, and bloody portions of what had been human beings before the iron horse had mercilessly rent them asunder; I have seen and helped to carry in my arms, children with their flesh in flames to their early deathbeds in the Infirmary, and suicides and fatal accidents have I seen over and over again ; yet in the midst cif these and other scenes of death, that which has most rivetted itself in my memory, and disturbed my imagination is one when as a boy I was not prevented from witnessing the death throes of an innocent sheep in a slaughter-ho Use. The vividness of that early scene returned with all its pristine intensity when in preparation for these papt re, for a second time in my life I stood in a slaughter-house and saw the scene repeated. Seeing that [had been so powerfully and lastingly affected by that scene, I was astounded beyond measure to find in going in and out amongst the children of the humble poor. in Warrington, that not only were they familiar with, but that they actually gloated over the agonies of these dumb animals. This revel- ation was not the least important factor in prompt- ing rue to thoroughly investigate the matter to the best of my ability. Suppressing names I will give the results briefly of one or two inquiries I casually made amongst these boys with whom other circum- stances bring me daily into closest contact so that I had not the slightest difficulty in obtaining what they were only too pleased to dilate upon with- out divulging what I was driving at. One grimy grinning youngster of ten or under tells me he goes into the slaughter houses for bladders, he likes to sae them knocking the cows down but, poor little chap, he is yet innocent and pare enough to say that he doesn't like to see them killing sheep. ,Ne- body says anything to him. Bigger boys laugh sarcastically when asked if they frequent the slaughter houses. Of course they do, Mr. So and So is very good at letting them in, he never says " nowt" and "it is grand fun." The men frequently ask them to hold the legs of Che sheep while they are being killed. And the boys say that some of the sheep kick prop- er hard and bleat very loudly. One of the boys says at first he used to turn away his head when ' the poor creatures are being stabbed but he does not do so now. One boy's idea of the best fun of all was when the men or the boys themselves daubed their faces and their hands in the blood but that idea gruesome as it is falls far short of another boy's idea. This imp of mischief, fortunately now for himself is in safe keeping in an Industrial school, although but turned eleven, thought that the best fun con- sisted in driving a pin into a bullock's eye, as soon as the animal had been klled and then seeing the fluid squirt out. He struck quite a theatrical and heroic attitude when he explained to the the other boys how it was done. These are a few, but surely sufficiently numerous examples of the many culled from bays who for the most part were the worst truants in town. I feel confident that a considerable portion of what should have been their school time was spent in these slaughter houses under circumstances which I have here described. There may be readers of these lines who are consol- ing themselves with the fact that such children only belong to the very poor and perhaps not the deserving poor. I admit the fart that generally speaking they do belong to this class, but I do not admit /hat this affords any grounds for consolation --rather the reverse. In course off my tour of in- spection, evidence was not wanting to show that children of the respectable classes were in danger of being contaminated. For instance one slaughter house has a large iron grating in the wall looking into a much frequented footpath. At this grating I foond a boy apparently of the respectable class, intently peering at what was going on inside, a bullock having been newly felled. I watched his counter,ance and could plainly tell h the aspect on his features that his nerves were strang to an un- natural tension and that his whole imagination was rivetted on the scene before him. The playground of one of the largest church schools in town is only separated by a wooden pailing of no great height from a slaughter house. Not only are the noises from the slaughter house plainly audible but it , would not take much of the usual, schoolboy ac- tivity to witness the whole proceedings from begin- ning to end, and we all know that curiosity is strong in boyhood. The outside stairs at the rear of the school overlook the slaughter house in ques- tion. The teachers and managers of that school must know perfectly well to what F allude, and! am save they will bear me out in saying it 18 a most undesirable neighbour. Other slaughter houses are overlooked by the windows of numerous dwelling houses, and the effects in their cases would be the eame. But corning again inside the building as 1 have previously stated in some cases, the dwelling houses of the slaughter house men are within the premises, and only is yard or two from ' the entrance to the killing shed. One little mite, scarcely able to more than toddle, left its door- step as soon as a bullock had been safely taken in- side the shed and secured, and stood an interested speetstor at the killing house door at what was go- ing on It was with very great reluctance thet the child was forced to leave, and I could well understand the truth of the remark that was jocularly passed by one of the men that there was nothing the child objected to more than to be taken away from witnessing the proceedings. Other instances of this occurred in the brief space of that oneafternoon. One other feature and then I've done with this phase of the question, and that is that during the hot months, if not indeed all the year round, theze are slaugeter houses whose doors are left open while the men ON at work, so that any boy or girl passing can with little or no effort se much that is not good either for their moral or their physical welfare. It is no wonder, therefore, to find boys of tender ages playing together in yards or open spaces at mock-killing and dressing each other as sheen or oxen, with an adroitness which shows that they have been eager witnesses and learners of what goes on in reality. I could preach and I could expatiate upon the consequences arising from the debasing influences to which the young are so freely exposed, but I will not. If the foregoing be not sufficient to arouse the indignation of the public I attribute it not to lack of description on my part, but to the hack of moral insensibility on the part of the public.

The Prince of Wales is a poor sleeper, and, aerator°, rises early. In this particular he resembles the Czar, ho Wren gets up at three in the morning. --4.-- PROPOSED NEW C EIAPEL IN LATCHFORD.

For a number of years, the Wesleyans of Latch- ford have agitated for a new chapel as their present preaching place does not belong to themselves butis a Temperance Mission. A site has now been chosen at the end of Thel wall-lane, and on Thursday meet- ings in support of the building fund were held in the Temperance Hall. The Rev. J. Kirtlan took the chair in the afternoon, and the meeting was ad- dressed by Miss Bessie Dutton. A public tea was afterwards held and then another meeting presided over by Mr. R. G. Ilenshall. During the course of the Meeting the Rev. J. liirtlan made the financial .statement which showed that including the day's proceeds of £l2 163.,the total amount already raised was ..£560. Mr. Percy Silcock, architect, was pre- sent and exhibited plans of the proposed new chapel which would seat 200 people, and will pro- bably cost about .£l,OOO or .£1,200. Addresses were also given by Mr. S. Moss who spoke in terms of praise of the Rev. J. Kirtlan for his energetic sup- port of the scheme, and Councillor Roberts who spoke as a Nonconformist in full sympathy with all sections of Nonconformity. He believed that there was plenty of room for the establishment of a very strong healthy Methodist came in Latchford, and expressed his intention of subscribing to the funds. He hoped that within 12 months, they might have an opportunity of meeting together in a very much Fenger place than they did that night.—The Rev. G. D. Mason, who made his first appearance in Latch- ford, gave an encouraging speech. and the meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the chairman and the pronouncing of the benediction.

THE TRADES UNION CONGRESS.

IMPORTANT RESOLUTIONS.

The twenty-sixth Trade Unions Congress was opened on Monday at Belfast. At the opening meeting the chair was taken by Mr. John Wilson, M.P. vice chair- man off the Parliamentary Committee. There was an unusually large attendance of delegates. Mr. Samuel Munro., president of the Belfast United Trades Council, was unanimously elected president, and Mr. Wilson resigned the chair to him.

The report of the Parliamentary Comniittee vim then presented by Mr Fenwick, M.P. Ainengst other mat- ters, the Committee noted with satisfaction the appoint- ment c f working men as magistrates and the reduction of the qualification of the guardians of the poor. The changes advocated by the Congress were all embodied in the Employers' Liability Bill, and the Committee trusted that every assistance would be given to the Gov- ernment in their endeavour to pass the bill this year. During the past year the relations between capital and labour had been severely strained. Effective organisa- tion had become imperative, and trade uni.mists were urged to make strenuous efforts to draw into their ranks the whole of the wage earners of the United Kingdom.

The Trade Unions Cmgress resumed its sittings at Belfast on Tuesday, when the President Mr. S. Monro delivered his address. He expressed a hope that this vt of the Congress to Belfast would hav&the benefical effect on the fortunes of Ireland. He believed that the trade unionism was destined to be the instrument to free Ireland from the incubus of religious bigotry and politi- cal intole,ance. He advocated increased labour repro- ..entation in Parliainent an extension of technical educa- tion, a reform of the poor laws, and the better organisa- tifin of women workers, and denounced strakes as crimi- nal folly when entered upon before all other means of settling trade disputes had been tried. Tne greater part 4.f the day was occupied with a dis- cussion of the Parliamentary Committee's report. vote of censure on Messrs. Fenwick,. and John Wilson, for their action in reference to the Miners' Hight Hours Bill was defeated. TYade unionists generally were recommended to give the• Miners Feder- ation all 2essible pecuniary support during the lock-out.

At Wednesday's sitting of the Trade Ithin,ns Congress, Mr. H. 'Mien moved a resolution to the effect that a voluntary fund should be established to assist Labour candidates at local and Parliamentry elections, each society desiring to affiliate with the movement to sub- scribe St. pet hundred members, and, all candidates re- ceiving financial assistance pledging thetnselves to sup- port the Labour programme. 'the resolution was sup- ported be Mr. J. H. Wilson, M.P.,. and Mr. B. Hardie, M.F. Mr. M'Donald (London) movad. an amendment affirming that candidates receiving_ financial assistance must pledge themselves to support th* principle of col- lective ownership and control of all means of produc- tion and distribution. He said the Labour members had no right to discuss party political questions while there-was a single man, woman,, or child in the coun- try suffering from want of work.. Mr. I'. Curran. (London), seconded the amendment. After a lengthy disclosion, the amendment was adopted by 137 votes to SV. . . _ _

Mr. Tiardie moved that imorder to assert the claims of the Labour party it was necessary that Labour members in the House of Commons shoula be unconnected with either of the political parties, and should sit in opposi- tion to any Government, " until strong enough to form a Labour Cabinet." The resolution was rejected. A resolution was passed expressing hearty approval of the Employers' Liabitity Bill, and urging its passage in, its.present form,_

At the Trade Umion Congress on Thursday Mr. Tillett moved a resolutia•ii calling upon the Parliannenatry Committee to formulate a bill for regulating the hours of labour, limiting them to eight per day, such bill to contain a c15114e enabling the organised members of .any trade protesting by billet against th? same to be ex- empted from its provisions. The resolution was sec.onded by Mr. K. Hardie, 111. P., end carried by a large major- ity. . _

A resolution calling for an amendment of the law re- lating to juries so that all workmen entitled toa vote at Parliamentary elections may sit as jurymen, with an allowance of 103. per day for their services, was moved by Mr. J. H. Wilson, M.P„ and adopted unanimously. The action of the Manchester Watch Committee in ordering the arrest and prosecution of persons taking collections on behalf of the lock-nut colliers was condem- ned in a resolution moved by Mr. Leonard Hall, such action being regarded by the Congress as "a glaring collusion betweouthe authorities and the employers to starve the men into an indefensible reduction of their waxes." . _

r "Penwick, M.P., and M. Neir Hardie, M.P„ were nominated for t le Parlimmtary secretarphip of the Congreasi

ELECTION OF COIIMISIONERA AT

RUNCORN.

The annual election of five members of the Run- corn Board of Improvement Commissioners took place at the Market Hall,on Thursday. There were ten candidates for the five seats, viz., Messrs. Saml. Taylor, E. Wynne-Humphreys, and Richard Abel, retiring members,and Messrs. Thomas Jones,Arthur Bough, Thomas Dean, Walter Collier, Wm. Mellor, Edwin Imison, and R. Roberts. To the regret of many ratepayers, Mr. W. Fisher,who has had a long connection with public affairs, did not seek re- election. More than ordinary interest was taken in the contest, and what is quite unusual, three meet- ings were held and the majority of the candidates addressed the electors. Mr. Humphreys gave an account of his stewardship injßethesdaLecture Hall on Monday, and was so successlul in explaining the position of the Board on several questions that had agitated the public mind that it was generally considered his chances of re-election were much im- proved. On Tuesday evening a meeting was held under the auspices of the Ratepayers Association, and speeches were made by Messrs. Imison, Mellor Collier, Humphreys, Abel and Roberts. The Associ- ation on Thursday issued a leaflet requesting the workingmen to vote for Collier;llumphreys,lmison, Mellor, and Taylor, " Who are pledged to study the welfare of the town." The bill concluded "Shake up the dry bones ! " Ratepayers were requested 'to vote for Abel, " the organiser of the soup kitchen" and a true friend of the workingmen. Mr. Collier sought the suffrages on the ground that he would work for general reform, while Mr. Humphreys claimed support on half a dozen grounds,most prominent being sanitary reform,aud the division of the town into wards, and because he was a true economist. Messrs. Imison and Mellor issued no squibs, while the remainder sought to be returned as independent candidates. Mr. Taylor, who at the present time fills the honourable posi- tion of chairman of the Board,issued a card bearing a portrait of himself. He drew attention his seven- teen years' service, and called upon the ratepayers to support their tried representative. The poll opened at nine o'clock, and it was found that Mr. F. Wiswall, the returning officer had made the most complete arrangements for the contest that the system of election would permit He was assisted by a staff, consisting of Messrs. F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Mashall (sec), M. Barker (surveyor), W. Sutton (assistant overseer). and J. I D. Jones (librarian). While Messrs. Percival, Garrett and Austin preserved order and directed the voters. Each candidate had a booth in the Market, Hall, and the voters as they entered the building were solicited for their votes most assiduously. The polling went on steadily throughout the day and there was an absence of excitement that made the contest appear much tamer than others of recent years. There was an old Commis- sioner in the Hall who attracted much notice. This was Mr. Shaw Brown, thirty years ago a leader of the Runcorn people, and now having fallen upon evil times, had revisited the scenes of his formt r glory. During the afternoon a collection was made on his behalf by some of the gentlemen present and a good sum was realised. The poll was closed at four o'clock and at a quarter to five the return- ing officer announced the result as follows : E .Imison 878

8. Taylor B2B E. Wynne Humphreys 675 W. Collier 662 Richard Abel 572 Thomas Dean 554 Thom is Jones 548 Arthur Hough 416 W. Mellor 400 R. Roberts

Short addresses were made by the successful and unsuccessful candidates, and on the motion of Nit% Taylor, seconded by Mr. Imison, a vote of thanks to the returning officer and his staff for the efficient way in which they had performed their duties was accorded.

The hundreds of ratepayers who had assembled then dispersed.

MARRIAGE OF A RUNCORN GENTLEMAN.

On Wednesday at Knutsford Parish Church, Miss Emily Kirkham, elder daughter of the late Mr. Geo. Kirkham, of Seven Oaks, was married to Mr. John B.Chadwicic, manager of Weston Point Salt Works, elder son of Mr. W. H. Chadwick, of Leinster Gar- dens, Runcorn. Both par ties are well known and considerable interest was displayed in the event, a number of the inhabitants sanding the pavements in the fashion peculiar to Knutsford. The bride who was attired in a travelling dress of grey tweed cloth with bat to match was attended by her sister Miss Maggie lifrkham, who wore a dress of ruby cloth, trimmed with cr&im silk. Both bride and bridesmaid carried beautiful bouquets, and the bridesmaid wore also a diamond and ruby ring the gift of the bridegroom. Mr. H. Chadwick,.brother of the bridegroom officiated as best man. The ceremony was performed by the Rev W. H. Barnacle, of Holmes Chapel, assisted by the Rev. G.L.Higson,and subsequently the happy couple received the congratulations of numerous friends. the honey- moon is being spent in the Lake district. The following is a list of the presents . .

'JUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

The attendance at the P.S.A. meeting- in Wyoliffe Church on Sunday afternoon was one of the largest I since its inaugeration. It was the first meeting in the second half year,and a lorgeaccessic.nto the membership was expected. Over seventy men pined, which brings the total membership.to TN. The chair was occupied by Councillrr Shaw Green. He expressed the great I pleasure it gave him to occupy the position of chairman that afternoon. He was glad to know that whilst attending to the spiritual welfare of the members, the society took an interest in social questions. He hoped that all listening to him would dO their duty in helping those who were trying to improve the social condition of the town. Mr. Biektnan in his address reviewed the first six. months work of the Wycliffe P.S. .A.N. society. He thought the workers in the society were to.be con. gratulated on the success which had attended their labours and that alt the members might "-Thank Gad and take courage.." He believed the secret of their success lay in the bond of sympathy which such. a society engendered and fostered amongst the brethren. An anthem was, well rendered by the. choir, Mr % A. Griffiths takinz the solo part. The Rew..T. Yonge. Mar. J. Drown, and Ur. G. Wallington also took part isathe meeting.

A GROCER'S PEG-U.I,IAR METHOD, OF

B USIN ES:4.

A grocer and provision dealer named Wynare, of ; Mostyn, Flintshira, and Whiston, Lancashire, under ex- amination in the Chester Bankruptcy Court. has given r tome extraordinary details ot his method of carrying on 1 business. It appears to have coasisted, as the Registrar observed, of obtaining goods froafthe wholesa/e houses. and handing them out to anybody who liked to come to the bankrupt's shop. One debtor to the estate was a collier named Jones, who had been allowed to run up a ' bill for 3118. He, it was admitted, had no weans of pay- ing,being " out of work among the strikers." Lyons, a file- entter. owed £l3l, and he too had no means. Jones, another collier, who owed MO% "could not pay at pre- sent, but promised instalments, "when the work start." Three other workingclass customers owed respectively £lOB, £l7O, and £lB6 "Have they anything ?" asked the Registrar ; to which the bankrupt's reply was" No, sir am sorry to say." Business, he added, " has 115- fortunately been very depressed, and they have not been able to do as I expected." Ttse net result is that Mr., Wynne's bad and doubtful dells amount to £7391,1 while his assets are valued at £l6O.

SAD SUICIDE 07 A CHESHIRE

SERVANT.

EARLY LOVE TROUBLES. Mr. Blunt, Deputy-Coroner for the Knutsford Division of Cheshire, held an inquest on Thursday at Mr. Arthur Ford's farm, High Legh, on the body of Catherine Chorlton, twenty years of age, a domestic servant. Mrs. Jane Cherlton, mother of the deceased, said her daughter cane to spend Sunday and Monday with her. She went to meet her sweetheart on the Sunday evening, but he did not turn up. DPCPASed left on Monday afternoon to return to her sister in Bowdon. About a .foitnight age she, had been very fretful, as her young man, Jos' I:0 Simpson, had been ill and told her he was falling iuto connimption. She had asked him to meet her at he! mother's house last Sunday, and he protnked to do so it he vere well enough. 11-ceasad had been weak for seise time back and had been unnerved by nursing a neigh- bour who was suffering from brain fever. Wm. Chorltql gave evidence of finding the body of deceased in a Pit 150 yards from her parent's door. Harriet Swindles. Bowdon, said deceased returned to her Imuse on It'•1 sr afternoon and seemed upset. During Tuesday she waS very hurried and excited at her work, and in the aUer- noon witness heard the front door close, and looking 00° of the window saw deceased hurrying away. Witness went to decea.ed's room and found a letter. •• All re& things are for my dear mother. This is the earl of a my troubles." Particularly during the past fortnight deceased had been very fretful. She was not a strong nerved girl, but apparently was striving hard t ) bear OP against her troubles. She was a very faithful and loc.& able girl.—Joseph Simpson, groom to Mr. T. P. Norrig• Lymm, said he had been keeping company with the deceased for the last eighteen months. She was veil fond of him, and there had not been any coolness on hi,,S part. He had been ill lately with bronchitis, and ha' on one or two occasions, for that reason, riot been able to fulfil his trysts with her, but he apprised her of it by letter. He could not account for her troubles, unless it was on account of his illness.—The jury returned a ver" dict of" Suicide whilst temporarily insane."
